--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page follows the dedication to the "Brides of Christ." Having completed the address to his readers, the author now provides a formal "Confession" or profession of faith. This was a standard practice for medieval and early modern spiritual writers to ensure their work was seen as orthodox and in line with Church teaching.</meta>

# CONFESSION
### of the
## AUTHOR

I, Brother JAN RUYSBROECK, Canon Regular, <note>A Canon Regular is a member of a Catholic religious community of priests who live together under a rule (such as the Rule of St. Augustine) and combine a monastic lifestyle with public ministry.</note> submit to the sentence and judgment of the Saints and of the Holy Church all that I understand, feel, or have written; for I wish to live and die a servant of Christ in the Christian faith. And I desire to be, through the grace of God, a living member <note>The original Dutch uses the word *lidt*, meaning a "limb" or "member," common imagery for the "Body of Christ."</note> of the Holy Church.

->**BROTHER JAN RUYSBROECK**<-
->**as above.**<-

<summary>In this formal statement, Jan van Ruysbroeck submits his entire body of work and his personal beliefs to the judgment of the Church, affirming his lifelong commitment to the Christian faith.</summary>
<keywords>Jan van Ruysbroeck, confession of faith, Canon Regular, Holy Church, orthodoxy, servant of Christ, submission to authority</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page provides the formal ecclesiastical approval (approbatie) for the work, following the author’s personal confession of faith. While Ruysbroeck wrote in the 14th century, this specific edition was vetted by the University of Toulouse in the early 17th century to ensure it met the standards of the Counter-Reformation Catholic Church.</meta>

# APPROBATION
### of the Ornament of the Spiri-
### tual Marriage.

We, the undersigned Doctors of the Faculty of Toulouse <note>original: "Faculteyt van Tolofen." The University of Toulouse was a prestigious center for Catholic theology in France.</note>, testify to have examined a booklet called THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE <note>original: "T’Cieraet der Gheestelijcker Bruyloft." This is the title of Jan van Ruysbroeck’s most influential work on the soul's journey toward God.</note>, in which we have found nothing contrary to the decrees of the Holy Roman, Catholic, and Apostolic Church, but rather, many beautiful and godly teachings, serving for the instruction of the Christian life. Given this 25th of September, 1605.

->B. Alvarus.<-
->J. Puteanus, Augustinian. <note>original: "I. Puteanus Augustinianus." An Augustinian is a member of the religious order following the Rule of St. Augustine, known for their focus on community and education.</note><-

<summary>This page contains a formal certificate of "Approbation" from the University of Toulouse, dated 1605, confirming that Ruysbroeck’s book is doctrinally sound and spiritually beneficial.</summary>
<keywords>approbation, University of Toulouse, orthodoxy, Catholic Church, spiritual marriage, censorship, 1605, J. Puteanus, B. Alvarus</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page follows the theological approbation from the University of Toulouse. It provides the official administrative "Permission" (Toelatinge) from the diocesan authorities (the Vicars General), who were governing the diocese after the Bishop's death, allowing the publication of Ruysbroeck’s work in 1606.</meta>

# PERMISSION.

WE, Vicars General <note>High-ranking administrative officials who govern a diocese, especially when the seat of the Bishop is vacant.</note> of the Chapter, having been chosen following the death of the Bishop, grant permission that the book named THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE <note>original: "T’Cieraet der Gheesttliicke Brvyloft."</note> written by the divine teacher and mystic <note>original: "schouyver," referring to one who practices "schouwen" or contemplative vision.</note> JAN RUYSBROECK, and translated into French by a Carthusian <note>A monk of the Carthusian Order, known for their extreme silence and solitude.</note>, may be published, having seen the testimony of the above-written Doctors of Divinity.

Given at Toulouse this last of February 1606.

*Bertrant Bertier, Archdeacon <note>A senior legal official in a diocese.</note> and Abbot of Lézat.*

*Antoni van Bruters, Provost of St. Stephen.*

*Niclaes Gilles, Canon and Theologian of St. Stephen’s.*

<summary>This page contains the formal administrative authorization for the publication of Jan van Ruysbroeck’s book, issued by the ecclesiastical authorities in Toulouse in 1606.</summary>
<keywords>permission, Jan van Ruysbroeck, Toulouse, Carthusian, Vicars General, ecclesiastical approval, 1606, printing license</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page provides a second official approval for the publication, this time from the religious authorities in Ghent in 1624. While the previous page showed the 1606 permission from Toulouse, this document authorizes a reprint with updated Dutch spelling and grammar to suit the readers of the early 17th century.</meta>

# APPROBATION <note>original: "APPROBATIO," a formal ecclesiastical endorsement certifying that a book contains nothing contrary to faith or morals.</note>
## Of the Censor.

Among all the high and spiritual teachings left behind by the very worthy and enlightened Doctor, Master JOHANNES RUYSBROECK, his books are exceptional, which he calls THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE <note>original: "T’Cieraet der Gheesteliicker Brvyloft."</note>; in which the beginning, the progression <note>original: "vvtganck," literally "going out," referring to the middle stage of the soul’s journey toward God.</note>, and the end of an exalted Divine life are appropriately, excellently, and very spiritually demonstrated and explained, for the help and solace of all those who seek nothing but God and rest in God. These, with some renewal and improvement of the old language according to the changes of the times <note>The 1624 editors updated Ruysbroeck’s 14th-century Middle Dutch to make it more readable for their contemporary audience.</note>, may be reprinted on this 4th of June, 1624.

->William Arents, Licentiate of Sacred Theology, Canon <note>A member of the clergy serving a cathedral.</note> and Archdeacon of Ghent. Censor of Books.<-

<summary>This page contains the formal ecclesiastical approval from the Censor in Ghent, dated 1624, authorizing a modernized reprint of Jan van Ruysbroeck’s mystical masterpiece.</summary>
<keywords>approbation, censorship, Jan van Ruysbroeck, Ghent, 1624, The Ornament of the Spiritual Marriage, William Arents, Dutch language history</keywords>

# THE FIRST CHAPTER:

*How a miracle occurred in him when he was only seven days old.*

<image-desc>An ornamental drop cap 'Q' featuring a decorative floral background.</image-desc>
<margin>
Henricus Pomerius, book 2, chapter 2.
</margin>
"Who do you think this child shall be?" <note>original: "Quis putas puer iste erit?" This is a biblical reference to Luke 1:66 regarding the birth of John the Baptist.</note> "Who do you think this child shall be?" spoke the people when the holy John the Baptist was born, because of the wondrous signs that occurred then; just as one might also have said in the year 1293, when the servant of the Lord, Jan Ruysbroeck, was born from the noble lineage of virtuous parents in <term>Brabant<note>A historic duchy in the Low Countries, spanning parts of modern-day Belgium and the Netherlands.</note></term>.

<margin>
Laurentius Surius, chapters 1 & 2.
</margin>
Whom God Almighty had destined from eternity for the great benefit of the holy Church, it pleased Him to honor his first days of childhood with a wondrous miracle.

<margin>
Johannes Gilemannus in *New Lives of the Saints*. <note>original: "in nou. Sanctorum." refers to the *Nova Sanctorum*.</note>
</margin>
For while still a young child about seven days old, when his nurse was about to wash him in a basin, he remained standing upright on his own legs in the basin through the power of God alone, without anyone holding him. ¶ The like of which one also reads as having happened to <term>Saint Nicholas<note>Saint Nicholas of Myra (4th century). Tradition holds that as an infant, he stood up in his bath for three hours in honor of the Trinity.</note></term>. But what shall I say of this wondrous miracle other than that it is a preceding sign of many wondrous things which our Lord—

<summary>This page begins the biography of the mystic Jan van Ruysbroeck, detailing his birth in 1293 and a miraculous event where, at seven days old, he stood upright in his bathwater unaided.</summary>
<keywords>Jan van Ruysbroeck, John the Baptist, Brabant, miracle, infancy, Saint Nicholas, hagiography</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>The previous page ended mid-sentence describing how the miracle of the infant Jan van Ruysbroeck standing in his bath was a sign of what the Lord intended for his future. This page continues that interpretation, explaining that his physical standing prefigured his spiritual elevation. Catchword: friends- (vrien-)</meta>

The Life

...would later work in this holy man? And among other things, that he would raise himself so high in the Spirit through divine contemplation that he would be uplifted therein beyond natural capacity, just as he remained standing upright here, so young, contrary to the common habit of other children.

## CHAPTER II.

->*How he, while still a young child of about twelve years, left his mother.*<-

<margin>Pomerius, chapter two. Surius, same chapter. Petrus Impens in the Chronicles. <note>original: "Prom. cap secundo. Surius eodem cap. Petrus Impens in Chro." Henricus Pomerius and Laurentius Surius were early biographers of Ruysbroeck; Petrus Impens was a 15th-century chronicler.</note></margin>
The aforementioned servant of God was also in part like <term>Saint John the Baptist<note>Ruysbroeck is often compared to John the Baptist because of his early inclination toward a solitary, ascetic life.</note></term> in this: that in his young days he strove to shun the pleasures of men. For this reason, he left his Mother—who loved him very dearly—while still a child of about twelve years. Led by the inspiration of God, he followed to the best of his ability those from whom he might learn good and disciplined virtues. Thus it happened that he came by chance to <term>Sir Jan Hinckaert<note>Jan Hinckaert was a priest and a cousin of Ruysbroeck who became his mentor and lived with him in a spiritual community.</note></term>, a <term>Canon<note>A member of the clergy who lives according to a rule (canon) and is attached to a cathedral or collegiate church.</note></term> of the Church of <term>Saint Gudula<note>The primary patron saint of Brussels. The Cathedral of St. Michael and St. Gudula remains a major landmark in the city.</note></term> living in Brussels. Who [received] him—

# Of Saint Jan Ruysbroeck.

...who, receiving him kindly, sent him to school; he followed these studies for about four years. Although he was of a sharp and capable mind, nonetheless—as one reads of <term>Saint Benedict<note>original: "H. Benedictus." Saint Benedict of Nursia (c. 480–547) famously abandoned his liberal arts studies in Rome to seek "well-taught ignorance," prioritizing spiritual growth over secular academic success. The author suggests Ruysbroeck followed this same path.</note></term>—he did not set his heart on attending school except where one learns **Divine wisdom**. 

But while living here with his cousin, the aforementioned **Canon**, he could not remain hidden from his mother. Having followed him there, she could not persuade him to return home with her; therefore, so that she might at least be able to see him daily, she came to live in the <term>Beguinage<note>original: "Beghijnhove." A community of Beguines, who were lay religious women living in a semi-monastic setting without taking permanent vows. The Great Beguinage of Brussels was a prominent religious center in the 14th century.</note></term> in Brussels. 

Furthermore, this same mother was more inwardly gladdened by the good reputation of his **holy life**, which reached her ears daily, than if she had lived with him according to her own natural desires. And this is no wonder: for the **Holy Spirit** unites those who love one another in God much more closely through the bond of His divine love (even if they are physically far apart) than if they lived together out of natural, human affection.

It also happened, when his mother had passed away, that while he remembered her daily in his prayers, she revealed herself to him and spoke to him very often in this manner, saying: "O dear child, will it be a long time yet before you shall become a Priest?" It then came to pass that, having become a Priest and performing his <term>first Mass<note>The "first Mass" (Primitiae) was a significant milestone for a new priest, believed to carry special spiritual efficacy and grace for those for whom it was offered.</note></term>, he was exceptionally comforted by our Lord, with the promise that his mother would henceforth be delivered from the pains of purgatory. ## CHAPTER X.

### How Master Gerard was greatly amazed by the immense confidence that Ruysbroeck had in God.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring vine-like leaf motifs and floral flourishes inside a square border.</image-desc>

<margin>Psalm 4:10 <note>In modern Bibles, this likely corresponds to Psalm 4:8: "In peace I will lie down and sleep, for you alone, Lord, make me dwell in safety."</note></margin>
As Master Gerard <note>Gerard Groote (1340–1384), a key figure in the Devotio Moderna movement, who often sought spiritual counsel from Ruysbroeck.</note> still used to visit this holy man from time to time, it happened once that he stayed with him at <term>Groenendaal<note>Ruysbroeck's monastery in the Sonian Forest near Brussels.</note></term> for a while. He did this so that his clouded understanding might be enlightened and that he might share in the fire of the <term>Prior's<note>Jan van Ruysbroeck.</note></term> burning love. At that time, it seemed to him—from what he could gather from the Prior’s words—that the Prior had less fear than he ought to have. For he found him so firmly united with God that the Prior used to tell him it was all the same to him whether he lived or died for the name of Christ, or even whether he felt the joy of Heaven or the pains of Hell, provided it was according to the beloved will of God. Upon hearing this, Master Gerard (who still felt a greater fear of the pains of Hell) was deeply moved and wondered greatly at this attitude. In order to move the Prior to a greater sense of fear, Gerard sought the help of many testimonies from Holy Scripture. He used these in an exhortation intended to instruct the Prior, arguing that it was not safe to rest in such great confidence in God’s goodness as if fear were entirely excluded. But the Prior, well know-

For I am entirely ready to receive from the hand of God whatever He wishes to send upon me; for nothing can be more pleasant or more agreeable to me, nor do I desire anything else, than that our Lord should always find me ready for His most beloved will."

O perfect obedience! O noble will! How true was the love and good trust in God coming from his heart!
<margin>Pomerius, chapter 11 <note>Henricus Pomerius was Ruysbroeck's first biographer; this citation refers to his Latin life of the saint.</note></margin>
It excluded that servile fear, but not without <term>filial reverence<note>Original: 'sonelijcke eerbiedinge'; the respectful awe a child feels for a parent, distinguished from the 'servile fear' of a slave.</note></term>. And why should this be wondered at, seeing that true love is as strong as death, which can bear all things equally? For love does not fear losing any profit, nor accepting any burden and discomfort, by which it might please the Beloved; rather, it only fears that which might cool or destroy the fervor of its love. That this is true we shall find through experience: after some grief follows some comfort; just as the Prophet says: *In the multitude of my <term>tribulations<note>Original: 'tribulatien'. Severe trials or sufferings that test a person's faith.</note></term>, my soul has been comforted.* <note>Original Latin paraphrase: 'secundum multitudinem dolorum meorum... consolationes tuae laetificaverunt animam meam'.</note> For although it is that the merciful Lord, when He tests His <term>chosen ones<note>Original: 'weuercozen' (uitverkozenen). Those selected by God for a special spiritual path or salvation.</note></term>, sometimes lets wonderful things come to them—great and unbearable tribulations according to human capacity—yet He comes quickly thereafter to the person's aid with great sweetness. And although He may sometimes wait a while, yet His sweet coming is so pleasant that the grief and the pains that one had before seem not to have existed at all, or seem so light and small compared to the greatness of the reward that follows in hope, that one might for that reason even desire to have such suffering. # CHAPTER XIII.

*How he had a marvelous manner of making his books.*

<margin>Pomerius ch. 14. Surius ch. 9.<note>Pomerius (Henricus Pomerius) and Surius (Laurentius Surius) were the primary biographers who preserved the history of Ruysbroeck’s life for later generations.</note></margin>
Pomerius says that it is also very much to be wondered at what our forefathers say and tell of him, namely the manner in which he composed his books; for he had a custom that when he was enlightened with the rays of the <term>Divine light<note>Original: 'Goddelijcken lichts'. In mystical theology, this refers to a direct spiritual illumination from God.</note></term>, he would go alone into a hidden place in the <term>forest<note>The Sonian Forest (Zoniënwoud) surrounding the Priory of Groenendaal near Brussels.</note></term>, and there he wrote on a <term>wax tablet<note>Original: 'wasse tafel'. A wooden board coated with wax, used as a portable notebook; text was etched with a stylus and could later be smoothed over and reused.</note></term> or slate all that the <term>Holy Spirit<note>Original: 'H. Geest'. Ruysbroeck’s followers believed his writings were divinely inspired rather than mere human compositions.</note></term> gave to him, which he used to bring with him when returning to the <term>Monastery<note>Original: 'Clooster'. Specifically the Priory of Groenendaal.</note></term>. And so, at various times, he made his books, notwithstanding that he was often for a long time and even some weeks deprived of that <term>grace<note>Original: 'gratie'. The specific spiritual gift or impulse required to write on divine matters.</note></term> of writing the Divine teachings; but at last, having returned to his previous manner, he very profitably and fittingly (yet without having memory of the previous things) continued the present with the preceding, just as if he had written them sequentially at one time. And although he was now becoming old, and his strength began to fail him, yet the...

### CHAPTER XIX.

->*How he was always ready to perform humble and*<-
->*lowly tasks.*<-

<margin>Pomerius, chapter 20; Chronicle of Brabant; Impens in the Chronicle.<note>These are citations to historical sources: Henricus Pomerius was Ruysbroeck's primary biographer; the *Chronicle of Brabant* and Peter Impens provided historical records of the region and the monastery.</note></margin>
This devout and holy Prior, offering himself as a fine example and for the spiritual edification of his brothers—specifically in fleeing from sin, in practicing virtues, and in observing the <term>monastic ordinances<note>original: "Cloosterlijcke ordinantie". These were the specific rules and daily regulations governing life within the Augustinian monastery of Groenendaal.</note></term> and statutes—such as working with his own hands, keeping vigils, and fasting, in which he surpassed all others. For when he occasionally used to work with his brothers, notwithstanding that he was old and weak of body, he nevertheless humbly devoted himself to heavy and <term>lowly labor<note>original: "vervvorpen aerbeyt". Literally "rejected labor," referring to tasks considered beneath a person of high status or office, such as manual waste removal, intended to cultivate humility.</note></term>, such as carrying manure and other simple and disregarded tasks which he performed. Because of this, it sometimes happened that through his diligent zeal and his simple innocence, he hindered his brothers—especially the <term>gardeners<note>original: "houeniers". The brothers responsible for the monastery’s kitchen and medicinal gardens.</note></term>—more than he helped them; for he did not know how to distinguish or recognize the weeds from the good plants, so that he pulled out the good herbs along with the bad—

In Christian tradition, Mary represents the "contemplative life" of prayer, while Martha represents the "active life" of service. A central concept in Ruysbroeck’s mysticism, referring to a state of internal unity or solitude where the soul is focused solely on God.</note></term>. So much so that he used to say that he could as easily lift his heart to God through contemplation as he could lay his hand upon his head.

## CHAPTER XX.

*How he had great compassion not only for people, but also for animals.*

How kind and well-mannered in speech, how <term>pious<note>original: "Godtvruchtich." A term describing one who is God-fearing and devoted to religious duties.</note></term> and humble this servant of God and follower of our Lord Jesus Christ was toward all his brothers was very evident, for he comforted all people and gladdened them with his presence. The grace of God shone and beamed from his face. In his words there was great <term>edification<note>original: "gestichticheyt." In a religious context, this refers to the spiritual improvement or "building up" of others through a good example or teaching.</note></term>; in his works, piety; in all his dealings, humility; and finally, in every aspect of his life, there was great goodness and the honor of the virtues. He was temperate in eating and drinking, <term>simple<note>original: "slecht." In this period of Dutch, this meant plain or unpretentious, rather than "bad" or "poor quality."</note></term> and humble in his clothing, patient in all suffering, and merciful toward all <term>creatures<note>original: "creaturē." In the medieval worldview, this encompassed all of God's creation, from humans to the smallest animals.</note></term>, so that he not only had compassion for rational creatures <note>A traditional theological term for human beings, who were distinguished from animals by their capacity for reason.</note>, but also for the birds and other animals. # of St. John Ruysbroeck.

<note>original: "Het," catchword from the previous page.</note>
...great obstacle forced him, against his will, to leave it aside. How much devotion he was ignited and filled with when he performed God’s service daily was quite evident. For once, as he stood during the <term>Canon of the Mass<note>The most solemn part of the Mass, containing the prayer of consecration where the bread and wine are believed to become the body and blood of Christ.</note></term>, he was (burning inwardly through the fervor of the Spirit and being showered with the dew of Heavenly grace) so melted by that sweetness that the natural life of his inner senses seemed to fail and leave him, so that he could hardly remain standing on his feet.

Because of this, his servant was very distressed and bewildered, so much so that he called someone else to fulfill and complete the Mass that had been started. But this did not happen to him because of any natural weakness or illness, but rather through the influence of divine grace; and although similar things happened to him often, he nevertheless never stopped <term>celebrating Mass<note>original: "Misse te lesen," literally "reading Mass."</note></term>.

For as he seemed to be entirely powerless in all his limbs and strengths, and almost without life, the servant who was assisting him thought it was impossible that he could have remained alive. to die, so that he was not at all afraid or terrified of death, because of the great cheer and joy that he showed through his outward actions and countenance; he very eagerly desired to die and to be with <term>Christ<note>original: "Christo."</note></term>. Therefore his blessed soul longed with a great, fiery desire (like a <term>deer<note>A reference to Psalm 42:1: "As the deer pants for streams of water, so my soul pants for you, my God."</note></term> for the waters of the springs) to fly to the lovely embrace of her <term>Bridegroom<note>original: "Bruydegom." When she had humbly followed this advice, she felt the pain immediately diminish, and shortly thereafter it passed away completely; and thus she regained her former health.

## CHAPTER XXXVI.

### *Concerning a certain tree called Ruysbroeck’s linden.*

[Decorative woodcut initial O featuring a stylized plant with a central flower or fruit within an ornate rectangular frame.]

About a bowshot <note>original: "booghscheut"; a traditional unit of distance representing how far an arrow could be shot, roughly 200 to 300 yards.</note> from the Monastery of Groenendaal <note>A significant Augustinian monastery in the Sonian Forest near Brussels, founded by Ruysbroeck and his companions in the 14th century.</note> in the forest, at the place where the worthy Ruysbroeck was found through the sign of heavenly fire (as was said above in Chapter 14), there still stands an old linden tree which is called **Ruysbroeck’s linden**. Twenty years ago—during the time when the Religious <term>Religious: A collective noun for members of a monastic order; in this case, the Augustinian canons.</term> of Groenendaal did not dare to live in their Monastery because of the constant war <note>Likely referring to the disruptions caused by the Eighty Years' War (1568–1648).</note>—this tree had so decayed that it had become completely hollow inside and was nearly nothing more than bark. However, when the Religious returned home, the tree began to grow back together and thrive, so that it now appears to be a new thicket <note>original: "bos"; here referring to the lush, dense growth of the tree's crown.</note> adorned with beautiful branches, the hollowness also having grown shut.

Near this tree, some were accustomed to go and perform their devotions <note>Acts of prayer or religious worship.</note> when they were troubled by certain ailments, which occurred before the relics of the worthy Ruysbroeck were elevated <term>Elevated: The formal ritual of "elevatio corporis," where the remains of a holy person were exhumed and placed in a shrine above ground, signifying official recognition of their sanctity.</term>. Among all these [instances], it happened

More than twenty years ago, during the time that the tree was still hollow, there arrived there for devotion the honorable maiden <term>daughter: original "dochter"; here used as a title for a respectable unmarried woman or a lay sister.</term> **Anna Smits**, together with **Father Livinus vander Elst** and **Sister Margarita Andriessens**. This Anna had unfortunately so injured or sprained <note>original: "verstupet"; an archaic term for a sprain or dislocation.</note> her foot that she could no longer walk upon it. Consequently, bystanders advised her that, trusting in the merits <note>In Catholic tradition, the "merits" of a saint refers to the spiritual credit they earned through a holy life, which can be invoked to intercede for others.</note> of the worthy Ruysbroeck, she should let herself be led or carried to Ruysbroeck's linden. Those present, taking her by the feet <note>original: "onder de voetsel nemende"; meaning they supported or carried her by her lower limbs.</note>, brought her to the linden tree. Calling upon the help of the worthy Ruysbroeck, they placed the injured foot into the hollow of the same tree. And it happened that she became faint and fell unconscious; but coming immediately to herself, she was healed of her injury and began to use her foot with great wonder, being entirely cured.

So that this tree would not be cut into pieces by the pilgrims in order to carry the wood away with them, her Highness the **Infante of Spain** <note>Isabella Clara Eugenia (1566–1633), the sovereign of the Spanish Netherlands, who was known for her piety and patronage of the Groenendaal monastery.</note> had the same enclosed <note>Likely referring to a fence or barrier built around the tree to protect it from being stripped for relics.</note>.

# CHAPTER XXXVI.

### *Concerning how certain eminent men have written about this worthy Father.*

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'D' at the beginning of the text, featuring ornate floral and scrollwork patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

The holiness of this worthy Prior has become so widely and broadly known that many eminent men (some of whom are also held to be Saints) have been unable to keep silent regarding his praise and honor; indeed, some out of devotion have described his entire life.

The first, then, who wrote his life was **Jan van Schoonhoven**, who in his time was the Sub-prior <note>A "Sub-prior" is the deputy or assistant to the Prior, the head of a monastery.</note> of **Groenendaal**, and who lived with this Holy Prior; he died in the year 1431.

The second was **Henricus Pomerius**, Prior of **Zevenborren** <note>original: "Sevenborre"; one of the daughter houses of Groenendaal located in the Sonian Forest near Brussels.</note>, who died in the year 1469.

The third was **Jan Gillemans**, Sub-prior of **Rooklooster** <note>original: "Rooclooster"; meaning "Red Cloister," another influential Augustinian monastery associated with Ruysbroeck's circle.</note>, who died in the year 1487.

Afterwards, **Peter Impens**, Prior at **Bethlehem near Leuven**, also wrote the same.

Likewise also **Joannes Mamburnus** <note>Also known as Jean Mauburn, a key figure in the Windesheim Congregation of the Modern Devotion movement.</note>, Abbot of **Livry** in France, in a book called *The Hunting-ground of the Order of Canons Regular* <note>original: "Venatorium Ord. Can. Reg."; Latin for "Venatorium Ordinis Canonicorum Regularium," a biographical and historical catalog of the Augustinian order.</note>. This man lived around the year 1500.

Dit <note>This "catchword" indicates that the first word of the following page (63) is "Dit" (This).</note>

<summary>This page catalogs the early biographers and historians who documented the life of Jan van Ruysbroeck, emphasizing the scholarly and monastic validation of his sanctity.</summary>

**Laurentius Surius**, a Carthusian <note>A monk of the Carthusian Order, known for their contemplative life and strict silence.</note> who translated the books of the worthy Ruysbroeck from Dutch <note>original: "Nederduytsch"; referring to the Middle Dutch language in which Ruysbroeck wrote.</note> into Latin, also added the life of this Holy man to them in the year 1552.

### The testimony of the good Cook.

**Brother Jan van Leeuw**, who because of his holiness is called the **Good Cook of Groenendaal**, and who, also being enlightened by God's Spirit, wrote very well and excellently about a spiritually elevated life, writes among other things that he saw this Holy Father <note>John Ruysbroeck.</note> elevated in such glory that at that time no one lived who surpassed him in merit.

### The testimony of Master Geert Groote.

**Gerardus Magnus** <note>Latin for Geert Groote, the famous founder of the Devotio Moderna movement and the Brethren of the Common Life.</note>, a holy man, writes in a letter to the Brothers of Groenendaal thus: "I commend myself deeply to the good grace of your Prior (Jan van Ruysbroeck), whose footstool I desire to be, both in this life and in the life to come; for my soul is attached to him with love and reverence more than to anyone else living."

<header>of St. Jan Ruysbroeck.</header>

...wondrous man Jan Ruysbroeck, who, being unlearned—so much so that he knew almost no Latin—has nonetheless, through the merits of his holiness and simplicity, described such high and supernatural, subtle meanings and truths in his mother tongue <note>Middle Dutch; it was rare and notable for such complex theology to be written in the common language rather than Latin.</note> that the highest and most learned theologians or **divines** <term>original: "Theologantē oft Godgeleerden"; scholars of religious doctrine.</term> are overwhelmed with wonder <note>original: "ontgheest"; literally to be "deprived of spirit" or rendered breathless by amazement.</note> and openly confess that they cannot understand or grasp his hidden and high meanings and sentences. 

And I likewise—the least, the smallest, and a newcomer in Christian science—confess and admit this openly: that in no ecclesiastical books of the Fathers and Doctors have I found such high teaching, nor any so difficult for me to understand, as in the books of this man. I only except the books of the great **Dionysius** <note>Referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian whose works were famously obscure and "dark."</note> which, because of the darkness of their style or manner of writing, are very difficult; however, such [intentional obscurity] has no place in the writings of this wondrous man.

Since this worthy Father <note>Dionysius the Carthusian, the author of these remarks.</note> writes so laudably of the worthy Jan Ruysbroeck, it also seems to me not without reason to include these following words, which he wrote in his defense against **Jan Gerson** <note>Jean Gerson (1363–1429), the powerful Chancellor of the University of Paris, who had criticized Ruysbroeck's work for being potentially heretical.</note>, formerly Chan-

<meta>Catchword: cellor; Ornament: fleuron centered below text</meta>

The text begins to address the historical theological dispute between Ruysbroeck's followers and Jean Gerson.</summary>

<header>The Life</header>

**Chancellor** <note>original: "lier"; completing the word "Kanselier" (Chancellor) from the previous page's "Jan G..." (Jean Gerson).</note> of Paris has written, to add here, and they are these:

The heartfelt or truly inward delight, through which the human **mind** <term>original: "ghemoet"; refers to the seat of thought, will, and emotion.</term> is pleasing to God, and the enjoyment through which the same mind enjoys the most supreme God, makes that mind very divine, God very lovable, and very embraceable—yes, so much so that it is found in the mutual, most lovely, **super-essential** <note>original: "overwezelijcste"; a term in mystical theology referring to a state of being that is beyond physical or finite existence.</note>, and most simple embrace of the Holy Trinity; and the Trinity in turn finds itself happily embraced by that same human mind. 

Thus, to love God above all, to cling to the most worthy one with love, to be united with Him, conformed to Him, and imprinted in Him, is to be **simplified into a God-like form** <note>original: "Godtformelijck vereenvuldicht"; the mystical process of stripping away worldly complexity to become one with God's simple nature.</note>, most nobly sanctified, and supernaturally established; and to be carried over into the truest affection of goodness, to be raised above oneself, to escape oneself, to expire in God, to flow away, to sink, to be **ecstatic** <note>original: "ontgheesten"; literally to be "out of one's spirit," describing a state of mystical trance.</note>, to die, and to be swallowed up in Him—becoming one or the same with Him, leading a God-assimilating life through a most lovely **imitation** <note>original: "naervolghinghe"; echoing the "Imitation of Christ."</note>, feeling Him living in the **ground of one’s soul** <term>original: "gront zijnder zielen"; the deepest, most interior part of a person where they meet God.</term>.

<meta>This page continues the defense of Jan van Ruusbroec’s mystical language, specifically addressing the radical terminology of "becoming one with God" used at the end of the previous page. The text explains that these terms are metaphors for the union of wills rather than a literal disappearance of the human soul into the Divine essence.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>of Saint Jan Ruusbroec.</header>

All these terms are to be understood not as the **un-becoming** <term>original: "ontwordinghe"; a mystical term for the stripping away of the self’s independent existence to be reformed in God.</term> of the created being, its total annihilation, or its transformation or transition into the Divine uncreated intellectual being; but rather as an improvement of the manner or way of being through a most noble and most excellent imitation of that same Divine Being. For all things are most closely aligned with that which they resemble, and they take the name of that whose actions they most closely follow. In this sense, we call wolves "gulpers" or "devourers" <note>The author uses a metaphor of animal behavior to show how we name things based on what they imitate or do, rather than their underlying substance.</note>.

Likewise, an intellectual being <note>referring to the human soul or mind.</note> is said to become one with that to which it is bound with the greatest love. For love carries the beloved over into the one who loves and makes both their wills one through a **conformity of willing and not-willing** <note>original: "gelijckformicheyt van willen ende niet willen"; a reference to the classical definition of friendship as *idem velle, idem nolle* (to want and not want the same things).</note>. Because of this, a friend is called "another self."

Furthermore, when someone leaves behind their natural or own way of working and no longer notices themselves, but follows the working of a higher or different nature, this person is said to pass away from themselves and to be changed into another, while nonetheless remaining what they have always been—just as—

<sig>C ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: likewise (original: "kerwijs", completing "gelijckerwijs")</meta>

<summary>This page clarifies Ruusbroec's mystical terminology, explaining that "becoming one with God" refers to a perfect alignment of the human will with the Divine through love, rather than the literal destruction of the soul's created nature.</summary>

in such a way that the Apostle says: *I live now, yet not I, but Christ lives in me* <note>original: "Ick leue nu voorts, niet ic. maer in my leeft Christus"; a translation of Galatians 2:20.</note>. And Solomon: *Turn away the ungodly, and they shall be no more* <note>original: "Keert om die goddeloose, ende sy en sullen niet zijn"; a reference to Proverbs 12:7.</note>. And thus we are also commanded to die to ourselves.

See, according to this manner of speaking, the worthy Father Jan Ruusbroec says very often that we become one and the same with God—one life, one salvation. Because of this, there has been one <note>This likely refers to the Parisian theologian Jean Gerson (1363–1429), who famously criticized Ruusbroec’s "The Spiritual Espousals" for appearing to promote pantheism.</note> who, paying more attention to the **husk** <note>original: "schuerse"; literally "bark" or "shell." This is a common medieval metaphor for the literal surface of a text as opposed to its spiritual "kernel."</note> of his words than to his meaning, writes that he intended to renew the error of those who said that the rational creature, through its elevation to God, was returning into that intellectual uncreated being—which was a gross and foolish error. Yet one must not think that the aforementioned Jan Ruusbroec wanted to confirm or renew this [error]; indeed, on the contrary, he writes very sharply against it. Nevertheless, he often has a way of speaking somewhat broadly <note>or "boldly," suggesting a lack of strict scholastic precision in favor of poetic or experiential descriptions.</note>, just as other Saints have also done, according to the greatness of their inwardness <term>original: "innicheyt"; a central concept in Rheno-Flemish mysticism referring to a deep, interior devotion.</term> and the fervor of their love. These are the words of the worthy Dionysius <note>Referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-6th century mystical author whose works on the "union with God" were foundational for later medieval mystics.</note>.

<summary>The author defends Jan van Ruusbroec against charges of heresy (specifically the "error" of pantheism), arguing that his radical language of union with God should be understood as a metaphor for spiritual fervor rather than a literal change in being.</summary>

### The testimony of the Reverend Leonardus Lessius of the Society of Jesus, a renowned writer in the Holy Church and Professor of Theology at the University of Louvain.

<image-desc>Decorative initial letter 'I' set within an ornamental square frame featuring foliate and floral motifs.</image-desc>

I, Leonardus Lessius <note>Leonardus Lessius (1554–1623) was a famous Flemish Jesuit theologian whose works on law and ethics were widely read across Europe.</note>, testify to having read various writings and the life of the most holy and enlightened man Jan Ruusbroec. His writings, according to my judgment, contain an exceptional teaching and light of the Holy Spirit: in such a way that the author of these must rightly be counted among the highest contemplatives <note>original: "schouwers"; those who practice the deep, quiet observation of God.</note> and princes of hidden divine knowledge <note>original: "verborghen Goddelijcke wetenschap"; a term for mystical theology.</note>.

Now regarding the exceptional holiness of his life, and the examples of the greatest virtues, from which I, among others, confess to have learned over fifty years ago: how good and blessed it is (as far as the state of my life permits) to dwell with oneself and with God—of which he was an exceptional teacher and a most holy practitioner. In such a way that it is piously to be believed

is <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: "...is piously to be believed that he..."</note>—that he has not only obtained the glory of the Saints, but also, both before and after his death, has been held in great wonder and reverence by many excellent men, and is counted among the Princes of the contemplative life <note>original: "schouwende levens"; referring to a life of deep prayer and focus on God rather than external work.</note>. For this reason, he is also frequently cited, and his testimony praised, by the reverend Father Alvarez de Paz <note>Diego Álvarez de Paz (1560–1620) was a Spanish Jesuit whose massive volumes on the spiritual life were standard textbooks for mystics in the 17th century.</note> (who very beautifully summarized the entire spiritual life in three parts). I pass over countless others who have always held this aforementioned Author in high esteem, and honored him as a most holy and most enlightened man, for they are well enough known.

It is also untrue that he draws the reader toward contemplation in such a way that he withdraws them from the exercise of virtues <note>original: "deuchden"</note>; anyone who reads him diligently will certainly feel otherwise, seeing as he describes the nature or properties of the virtues in an excellent and very precise manner, and very often emphasizes their necessity. I confess, however, that reading him is not suitable for all people, and especially not for the young, because of the height <note>original: "hoocheyt"; meaning the sublimity or intellectual difficulty.</note> and obscurity <note>original: "donckerheydt"; a term often used in mystical theology to describe divine truths that are "dark" only because they are too bright for human reason to grasp.</note> of various meanings that are taught there.

# The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage, between God and our nature, composed by the enlightened visionary Master John Ruysbroeck, first Prior of the Monastery of Groenendaal. <note>Jan van Ruysbroeck (1293–1381) was a Flemish mystic. This work, *Die Geestelike Brulocht*, is considered a masterpiece of Christian mysticism. Groenendaal was his monastery near Brussels.</note>

## To the Pious Reader.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'H' containing stylized foliate and floral motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>
It is truly a lamentable thing that man, who is created for such a noble and exalted end as is (according to the words of St. Thomas <note>St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential Catholic philosopher and theologian.</note> and many other teachers) to know that supreme Being, as much as a creature is able to know, and to cling to the same with a constant love, in which his highest salvation <term>saelicheydt</term> <note>This term encompasses both earthly happiness and eternal blessedness/salvation.</note> is found: yet in such a way he loses himself in created things, which are but a reflection and a spark of that same Being, such that he places a great intermediary <note>original: "middel"; here referring to a distraction or something standing in between the soul and God.</note> between himself and this Divine Being

<summary>This preface introduces Ruysbroeck's treatise on the spiritual marriage, lamenting how humanity often loses sight of its divine purpose—knowing and loving God—by becoming distracted by created things.</summary>
<keywords>John Ruysbroeck, mysticism, Spiritual Marriage, Groenendaal, Thomas Aquinas, salvation, divinity, creation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 69. The word "tus-sen" (between) concludes here, referring to the "great intermediary" mentioned previously that stands between man and God.</meta>

## To the Pious Reader

between, which robs him of this true knowledge and constant clinging, in which, as previously stated, all his rest lies; not noticing that the creature <note>In this context, "the creature" refers to any created thing or being in the material world, as opposed to the Creator.</note> is too narrow and too cramped to fill his heart and to give him this true rest. This rest is not to be obtained except through a withdrawing and a separation of the senses and the thoughts from the pleasant apple of this world <note>A metaphorical reference to worldly temptations, echoing the forbidden fruit of Eden.</note>, through a constant annihilation, declining, and dying of the earthly man. This is exactly what many Holy Men, enlightened by God’s rays, have done, and thus they have found and discovered within themselves this true rest, which is God Himself.

These men, through a true compassion springing from Divine love, have left us suitable means in their writings to attain this aforementioned rest. Among whom, not the least, but indeed one of the greatest, was the Divine visionary <term>schouwer</term> <note>Literally "beholder." In mystical theology, a *schouwer* is one who practices contemplative prayer, "beholding" divine truths beyond intellectual reasoning.</note> Master John Ruysbroeck. He is very renowned both through his lofty writings and through his edifying and Holy life, and he is greatly praised by many enlightened teachers, but especially by the enlightened visionary Dionysius the Carthusian <note>Dionysius the Carthusian (1402–1471) was a famous theologian and mystic who was a great admirer of Ruysbroeck’s work.</note>. Dionysius marveled at this man because he was little experienced in human science <note>Refers to formal academic or scholastic education.</note> which is brought in from the outside through the senses. A reference to 1 John 2:27, emphasizing inner spiritual teaching over external schooling.</note> And that this is so, we also find in his life; for having once been asked by that great Master **Gerardus Magnus** <note>Also known as Geert Groote (1340–1384), the founder of the Brethren of the Common Life and a key figure in the *Devotio Moderna* movement.</note> why he sent forth such lofty books, seeing that he was unlearned, and also that some took offense and were scandalized by his writings?

Which words some brothers who lived with him declared they had heard from the mouth of this Holy Father, and received as a testament at the end of his life. 

Seeing and considering this well, it seemed good to me to set before you and publish this present book for the common profit of all hungry souls; it very briefly teaches the beginning, the progress, and the end of the entire spiritual life, wherein this true rest is hidden. And notwithstanding that there are many books of old that speak of this life—such as a St. Dionysius, Gregory, Augustine, Leo, Bonaventure, and many others—and daily new ones come forth, like Tauler <note>Johannes Tauler (c. 1300–1361), a famous Dominican mystic.</note>, Harphius <note>Hendrik Herp (d. 1477), a Franciscan whose mystical writings were widely influential.</note>, Benedict,

### To the Pious

**John of the Cross** <note>original: "Ioannes à Cruce." A Spanish mystic and reformer (1542–1591), known for "The Dark Night of the Soul." His inclusion here suggests this preface was written after his works became widely known in the late 16th or early 17th century.</note>, and suchlike; yet, because it sometimes happens in these spiritual matters just as it does in physical matters—namely, that what tastes good to one is unpalatable to another—and also because I see that so many books are being published which contain nothing but a multitude of fine words joined together, and many new inventions that are pleasing to the intellect but distract <term>verstroyen</term> <note>In a mystical context, "distraction" or "scattering" refers to the mind being pulled away from its single-minded focus on God toward a variety of worldly or intellectual thoughts.</note> the person more than anything else: for these reasons, I have not wanted to withhold from the zealous and desirous soul this precious little book. It offers a divine teaching and brings the human being back to that single end for which he was created: namely, to a true union with God. 

I only pray that no one takes offense at any lofty and hidden meanings they shall find here, but rather brings to this reading a good will to practice virtue, honoring that which they cannot understand or grasp due to its obscurity <note>original: "duysterheyts," literally darkness.</note>. One should truly believe that the Saints have left behind even higher things, and have hidden many rays of their divine knowledge, fearing that the weak eyes of men would not be able to endure them. And to tell the truth, it is no wonder that these things seem strange to some people who still live according to the flesh and have not put on the **new man** <note>A reference to Ephesians 4:24, referring to the spiritual transformation of a person through grace.</note>, who is created after God’s image...

He warns readers not to be discouraged by obscure or difficult passages, as divine truths are often too bright for "weak eyes."</summary>

The Adornment <note>original: "Het cieraet," referring to the title of the work, *The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage*.</note>

# CHAPTER I.

### How Christ teaches four things in these words, and how they are explained through three ways of life.

<margin>Matthew 25</margin>
Now speaks the Master of Truth, Christ: "Behold, the **Bridegroom** <term>Bruydegom</term> comes, go out to meet Him." <note>This central verse from the Parable of the Ten Virgins serves as the foundation for the entire book.</note> In these words, He teaches us four things: First, He gives a command when He speaks the word "Behold"; and all those who remain blind and neglect this command are condemned. In the second word, He shows us what we shall see—namely, the coming of the Bridegroom. Third, He teaches and commands us what we shall do when He says: "Go out." <margin>How our life must be.</margin> In the last words, where He speaks of "going out to meet Him," He demonstrates to us the profit and usefulness of all our works and our life, which is a loving meeting with the Bridegroom. We wish to interpret and unpack these words in three ways. First, according to the common way of a "beginning life," which is called the **active life** <term>werckende leven</term> <note>The "active life" refers to a state of being focused on outward good deeds and the basic requirements of faith.</note>, which is necessary for all people who wish to be saved. Second, concerning an interior, elevated, and yearning life, which many people attain through the virtues

<summary>This page introduces Chapter I, focusing on the scriptural call "Behold the Bridegroom comes," and outlines four lessons within that call while introducing the "active life" as the first of three spiritual stages.</summary>

### CHAPTER II.

### Of the active life.

<margin>Christ is the wisdom of the Father.</margin>
Now then, Christ—the wisdom of the Father—speaks in the first place; and according to His Divinity, He has spoken inwardly to all people from the time of Adam onwards, saying: "Behold." And this seeing is necessary.

<margin>Three things that one must have to see physically.</margin>
Now observe with diligence that for whoever would see, whether physically or spiritually, three things are required. The first is: if a person is to see physically from the outside, he must have outward light from the heavens, or other material light, so that the medium—namely the air—may be illuminated through which he shall see. The second is a willingness <note>original: "vrijvvillicheyt," implying a voluntary opening of the senses</note> that he allows the things he shall see to be mirrored <note>original: "herbeelden," literally to 'image again' or reflect</note> in his eyes. The third is that the instruments—namely the eyes—are healthy and without blemish, so that the coarse, physical things may be subtly mirrored therein. If he lacks any of these three, his physical sight fails. Of this seeing [we] will...

The prevenient grace <note>original: "voorgaēde gratie," the grace that "goes before" or precedes human effort, nudging the soul toward God</note> touches all people, for the Spirit of God is common <note>original: "ghemeyn," meaning universal or shared by all</note>; but a free turning and the purification of the conscience are not given by all people, and therefore they lack the grace of God through which they might earn eternal life.

<margin>
Of prevenient grace.
</margin>
The prevenient grace of God touches people from without or from within. From without, it happens through sickness, the loss of temporal goods, of kin or of friends, or through public shame; or else through preaching, the good examples of the Saints and of good people, or indeed through their words or works, so that through these things a person comes to know themselves. This is that touching of God from without.

<margin>
How one comes to the knowledge of oneself.
</margin>
Sometimes a person is also touched from within through the contemplation of the sufferings of Christ <note>original: "pijnen Christi"</note> and of the good that God has done for them and for all people; or by considering their sins, the shortness of life, the fear of death and of hell, and the joy of Heaven; furthermore, that God has spared them in their sins and waits for their conversion <term>bekeeringhe</term> <note>a turning around of one’s life and will toward God</note>; or indeed, they observe the wonder that God has created in the kingdom of Heaven, on earth, and in all creatures. ## THE III. CHAPTER.

### Of the grace that makes us pleasing to God and unites him with us.

When man has thus done his best and can go no further because of his own frailty <note>original: "cranckheyt," referring to the inherent limitations or "sickness" of human nature without divine aid</note>, then it pertains to the bottomless goodness of God to complete this work. Then a higher light of God's grace arrives, just like a flash of the sun, and is poured into the soul without merit <note>original: "onuerdiēt," meaning not earned or deserved by the soul's own power</note> and without being desired according to worthiness. For in this light, God gives Himself out of pure goodness and generosity—that which no creature can earn before they possess it. And this is a secret working of God in the soul beyond time, which moves the soul with all its powers; here the prevenient grace <term>voorcomende gratie</term> <note>God's initial grace that prompts a person to turn toward Him before they have even asked for it</note> ends, and here the other grace begins—that is, this supernatural light. This light is the first point, and from this springs the second point, which is from the soul's side, namely: a free turning of the will in a single moment of time. And there charity <term>charitate</term> <note>divine love or "caritas," the highest form of love that unites the soul to God</note> springs forth in the union of God and the soul; these two points hang together, so that one cannot be fulfilled without the other.

<summary>This page describes the transition from human preparation to divine intervention, where God’s "supernatural light" enters the soul to complete the work of salvation. This light triggers a "free turning of the will" and creates a mystical union through the gift of charity.</summary>
<keywords>Jan van Ruusbroec, supernatural light, charity, free will, divine union, sanctifying grace, human frailty, spiritual marriage</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul's union with God. ->of the Spiritual Espousals.<-

Where God and the souls gather in the unity of love <note>original: "minne," a Middle Dutch term for mystical or divine love, often implying a deep, mutual affection between the soul and God</note>, there God gives His light of grace beyond time, and the soul gives its free turning <term>vryen toekeer</term> <note>the soul’s voluntary response or conversion toward God made possible by grace</note> through the power of grace, in a brief "now" of time <note>original: "cortnu des tijts," referring to an instantaneous moment that bridges time and eternity</note>. And there charity <term>charitate</term> <note>divine love or "caritas," the theological virtue that unites the soul to God</note> is born in the soul from God and from the soul, for love is a bond of affection between God and the loving soul. 

From these two points—namely from grace and from the free turning of the enlightened will with grace—springs charity, which is Divine love. And from Divine love sprouts the third point, namely the purifying of the conscience: these three points run together, such that one cannot exist for any amount of time without the others. For whoever has Divine love has complete repentance for their sin. 

<margin>D</margin>
Yet one may understand here the ordinance <note>original: "ordinantie," referring to the divine order or structured relationship established by God</note> of God and of the creatures, as is demonstrated here: for God gives His light, and through that light, man gives the willing, complete turning. From these two sprouts complete love toward God, and from love comes complete remorse and the purifying of the conscience. And that occurs in looking down upon the misdeeds and upon the stains of the soul. And because he loves God, there arises in him a displeasure with himself and all his works.

## CHAPTER VIII.

### Concerning the third manner found in Christ, namely: suffering in patience until death.

What

The third point which we should observe with diligence in Christ is His patience <term>verduldicheijt</term> <note>This term refers to the virtue of "patient endurance" or "long-suffering," particularly the ability to remain steadfast under the weight of trials.</note> in all suffering, for it adorned Christ our bridegroom throughout His entire life. He fled into a foreign land <note>This refers to the Flight into Egypt to escape King Herod.</note>; He served Joseph and Mary His mother; He suffered hunger and thirst, shame and contempt, and the disgraceful words and deeds of the Jews. He fasted, kept watch <note>original: *waeckte*, referring to spiritual vigils or staying awake in prayer.</note>, and was tempted by the enemy <note>In this context, the "enemy" is the devil.</note>. He was subject to all people; He went from one land to another with great labor and diligence to preach the Gospel. Finally, He was captured by the Jews, who were His enemies while He was their friend; He was betrayed, mocked, scourged, and condemned by false witnesses.

### CHAPTER IX.

#### How God comes into us with new graces.

<margin>Christ is always ready to give himself to us.</margin>
The other coming of Christ our Bridegroom happens daily in good people, often and many times, with new graces and gifts that are added to them according to their capacity <term>vermogen</term>. Here we do not wish to speak of the first conversion of the person, nor of the first graces that were given to him when he first turned from sins to virtues, but rather of an increase in new gifts and virtues from day to day, and from

to the present coming of Christ our Bridegroom daily in our soul. Now it remains for us to observe the cause, the wherefore, the manner, and the works of this coming. There are four causes. God’s mercy, our necessity <term>nootdrufticheydt</term> <note>This refers to our inherent spiritual poverty and total dependence on God.</note>, God’s generosity, and our longing. These cause the virtues to grow in nobility. Now we must use a comparison, so that you might understand me better. 

When the sun casts its rays and brightness into a deep valley between two high mountains, and stands at the highest point of the firmament so that it may shine upon the bottom and the ground of the valley, then three things happen there. First, the valley becomes brighter and reflective from the mountains; second, it becomes more heated; and finally, it becomes more fruitful than a level land <term>effen lant</term> <note>The OCR reads "essen lant," likely a transcription error for "effen lant" (flat or level land). ## CHAPTER X.

### How one daily increases by means of the Holy Sacraments.

<margin>The coming of Christ through the receiving of the Sacraments.</margin>
There is yet another coming of Christ our Bridegroom <note>Ruusbroec’s central metaphor, where the human soul is the bride and Christ is the husband.</note> which occurs daily for the increase of grace and new gifts. This is when a person receives any Sacrament with a humble heart / and without any obstacle <term>contrarieteyt</term> <note>original: "contrarieteyt." In medieval theology, this refers to an "impediment"—a spiritual state like lack of faith or unconfessed sin that blocks the grace of the sacrament from working.</note> to the Sacrament / then they receive new gifts and greater graces because of their humility / and through that secret working of Christ in that Sacrament. The obstacle to the Sacrament is unbelief in Baptism / no repentance in confession / and to go with mortal sin or an evil will to the Sacrament of the Altar <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note> / and so forth for the other Sacraments. These people receive no new grace / but sin much more. This is the other coming of our Bridegroom which is daily present to us / this we should observe with a longing heart / so that it may happen within us. For this is necessary / if we are to remain standing in virtue / or go forward into eternal life.

<summary>Ruusbroec explains that Christ "comes" to the soul daily through the Sacraments, provided the recipient is humble and lacks spiritual obstacles like unbelief or mortal sin.</summary>
<keywords>Sacraments, Grace, Humility, Baptism, Confession, Eucharist, Bridegroom, Spiritual growth</keywords>

## CHAPTER XI.

### On the third coming of Christ for Judgment.

<margin>Three reasons for the third coming of Christ within us.</margin>
The third coming, which is yet to come, is for Judgment, or indeed at the time of each person’s death. The causes of this coming are the fitness of the time, the propriety of the matters, and the justice of the judge. The fitness of this coming is the last hour of death and the last day of Judgment. For when God created the soul from nothing and united it with the body, He appointed for her a set time—known to Him alone—when she should leave that body and must appear before His face. The propriety of the matters <note>This refers to the "fittingness" or necessity of the event based on the nature of human life.</note> is because the soul must give an account before the eternal truth for all the words and works she ever performed. The justice of the judge is because that judgment and sentence belong to Christ, for He is the Son of Man and the wisdom of the Father; to which wisdom all judgment belongs, for to her all hearts in Heaven, on earth, and in hell are clear and manifest. And these are the three causes of the general coming of Christ on the Last

<summary>Ruusbroec outlines the "third coming" of Christ, which occurs both at an individual's death and at the final Judgment Day, explained through the lenses of divine timing, human accountability, and Christ’s absolute wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>Last Judgment, death, Christ as Judge, divine wisdom, accountability, soul and body, end of days</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 78 regarding the third coming of Christ, focusing on the mechanics of the Last Judgment and the particular judgment at the moment of death.</meta>

->of the Spiritual Espousals.<-

Day; and also of the particular coming to every man in the hour of his death.

<margin>Our works shall be judged according to the intention.</margin>
The manner which Christ our Bridegroom and judge shall use at the Judgment—that is, to reward and to punish according to justice—is that he shall give to all men according to their merits. To the good, he shall give for every good work that was offered to him with a <term>right intention<note>In Ruusbroec’s theology, the "intention" (meyninghe) or inner motive is more important than the external act itself.</note></term> an unmeasured reward that no <term>creature<note>Ruusbroec uses "creature" to refer to any created being, specifically human souls in this context.</note></term> could earn by its own power—which is himself. But because he works that work together with the creature, the creature earns God himself as a reward through His power, according to fitting justice. To the damned he shall give eternal woe and eternal pain, because they have despised and rejected an eternal good for one that is perishable, and because they have freely turned away from God against his honor and will, and have clung to created things; therefore they are justly condemned. The witnesses who shall appear in this Judgment are the Angels, the conscience of man, and the enemy from hell; and the judge shall be Christ, whom no one can deceive.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Ruusbroec explains that Christ judges based on the "right intention" of the heart, rewarding the faithful with the gift of Himself and condemning those who prioritized worldly things over the eternal.</summary>

## THE XII. CHAPTER.

### Of five various kinds of people who shall come to judgment.

Five kinds of people must appear before this judge. The first and worst are the Christians who die in <term>mortal sin<note>In medieval theology, a "mortal sin" is a grave offense that destroys the relationship with God and leads to eternal damnation if the person does not repent before death.</note></term> without repentance and <term>contrition<note>The original text uses "berouw ende leetwesen." While both mean "remorse," Ruusbroec distinguishes between the turning of the mind (repentance) and the inner sorrow of the heart (contrition).</note></term>; for they have despised the death of Christ and his Sacraments, or received them in vain and improperly. They have also not performed the <term>works of mercy<note>These are traditional acts of charity, such as feeding the hungry or sheltering the homeless, which were considered essential evidence of a living faith.</note></term> toward their <term>neighbors<note>original: "euennaesten" — literally "those who are equally near," emphasizing the shared humanity of all people.</note></term> according to God's commandments; and for this reason they are condemned to the very depths of hell. The others are unbelieving Heathens or Jews who must all appear before Christ; nevertheless, they were condemned all their lives, for they never had grace or <term>Divine love<note>original: "Goddelijcke minnen" — "Minne" is a key technical term in Dutch mysticism for the ecstatic, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note></term>. Therefore they lived always in the eternal death of damnation; but they shall be tormented less than the evil Christians, because they have received fewer gifts from God, and also owe less faithfulness to God. The third are good Christian people who sometimes have fallen into sins and risen again through repentance and contrition

<meta>catchword: contrition</meta>

<summary>Ruusbroec begins a classification of five types of people at the Last Judgment, arguing that unfaithful Christians receive the harshest punishment because they squandered the specific graces and sacraments they were given.</summary>
<keywords>Last Judgment, mortal sin, repentance, contrition, Sacraments, works of mercy, Heathens, Jews, Divine love, damnation</keywords>

<margin>
How we must follow Christ’s first coming, long for the second, and await the third.
</margin>

The first coming—that God became man, and that he lived in <term>humility<note>original: "ootmoedicheydt" — a core virtue in medieval mysticism, referring to the soul's recognition of its own nothingness in relation to God's greatness.</note></term> and died in love for our sake—this we shall follow outwardly with perfect habits of virtue, and inwardly with <term>charity<note>original: "charitate" — derived from the Latin *caritas*, meaning the highest form of Christian love.</note></term> and with true humility. The second coming, which is present—namely, when he comes with grace into the loving soul—this coming we shall desire and daily pray for, so that we may remain standing and increase in new virtues. The third coming at the <term>Judgment<note>original: "Oordeel" — referring specifically to the Last Judgment at the end of the world.</note></term>, or in the hour of our death—this we shall await with longing, with confidence, and with reverence, so that we may be released from these miseries and may come into the hall of glory. This "coming" in these three manners is the second point of the four principal points that are mentioned.

# CHAPTER XIII.

## Concerning a Spiritual Going-Out in All Virtues.

<margin>
What is required to see spiritually.

Now he further commands us what we shall do and says: "Go out." If you have seen that first point—that you are seeing in grace and in charity—and if you have also rightly paid attention to your exemplar, Christ, and his "going out": then there springs up in you, from charity and from this contemplation of your Bridegroom, a <term>righteousness<note>original: "gherechticheyt" — in this context, it refers to a moral rightness or the active alignment of the soul's will with God's will.</note></term>; that is, that you desire to follow him in virtues. Then Christ speaks in you: "Go out." This <term>going out<note>original: "vvtgaen" — a central concept in Ruusbroec's mysticism (Latin: *exitus*). It describes the soul's movement from its own self-centeredness into the world through virtuous action and toward God in love.</note></term> must happen in three ways: for we must go out toward God, toward ourselves, and toward our fellow neighbors; and that with humility, with love, and with righteousness. Love always hunts upward toward the kingdom of God, which is God himself. For he is the source

For charity always holds man before the <term>bottomless goodness<note>original: "grondeloose goetheyt" — a signature phrase of Ruusbroec, referring to the "abyssal" or infinite nature of God's essence.</note></term> of God from which she flows out, so that man may thus live honorably before God and may persevere in virtues, and increase in love and in all true humility. Righteousness holds man before the eternal truth of God, so that he may discover it and may be enlightened, and thus fulfill [his tasks] without straying; but humility always holds man before the high majesty of God, so that he may always remain small and lowly, and trust in God, and hold nothing of himself. This is the manner in which man must conduct himself before the eyes of God, so that he may always increase in new virtues.

## CHAPTER XIV.

### How humility is a foundation of all virtues.

<margin>description of humility</margin> Since we have said that <term>humility<note>original: "ootmoedicheyt" — literally "lowly-mindedness" or "downward-heartedness."</note></term> is a foundation of all virtues, it is more than reasonable that we begin with it. Humility is as much as to say lowliness of mind or depth of mind; that is, an inward bowing down or inclining of the heart and the mind before the high dignity of God. This is commanded and ordered of us by <term>righteousness<note>original: "gherechticheyt" — the virtue of giving God and others their due.</note></term>, for through charity the loving heart cannot leave this undone; for when the humble and loving person considers that God has served him so humbly, so lovingly, and so faithfully, and that He is so high, so mighty, and so noble, while he [man] is so poor, so small, and so low, then there is born in that humble heart a

<margin>How much reason we have to be humble</margin> 

very great reverence and honor for God. For to show respect to God with all works, from within and without, is for humility the most pleasing thing, and for charity the most <term>savory<note>original: "aldersmaeckelijckste" — Ruusbroec often uses taste-based metaphors (savory, delicious) to describe the soul's experience of spiritual truths.</note></term>, and for righteousness the most proper; for the loving, humble hearts cannot [withhold honor from] God

<meta>catchword: not</meta>

<summary>In this chapter, Ruusbroec defines humility as an internal "bowing down" of the soul caused by the contrast between God's majesty and human smallness. He argues that humility, charity, and righteousness work together to lead a person to honor God in all things.</summary>

And he is also humble and shows great reverence to the Holy Church and the Sacraments.

And he is <term>contented<note>original: "ghenoechsaem" — meaning satisfied with what one has, or sufficient in oneself through God.</note></term> and moderate in food, in drink, in words, in his conduct, in his <term>dress<note>original: "habijt" — while this can mean a monk's habit, in this context it refers to general clothing and outward appearance.</note></term>, in his manner of answering, and in a humble expression without hypocrisy or affectation. He is also humble, outwardly and inwardly, before God and all people, so that no one is offended by him. 

And in this way, he overcomes and drives away <term>pride<note>original: "hooverdicheyt" — the vice of arrogance or self-exaltation, considered the root of all sin.</note></term>, which is a cause and the beginning of all sins. Through humility, the snares of the enemy, of sin, and of the world are torn apart; and man is <term>rightly ordered<note>original: "gheordincert" — a key term for Ruusbroec, meaning to be aligned correctly with God's divine order.</note></term> within himself and set in the proper place of the virtues. 

Heaven is opened to him, and God is inclined to hear his prayer; he is filled with grace, and Christ, the firm stone, is his foundation. He who builds virtues upon that foundation in humility cannot go astray.

## CHAPTER XV.

### On Obedience.

<margin>From where obedience comes.</margin>
From this humility springs obedience, for no one except the humble alone can be inwardly obedient. Obedience is a lowly, submissive, and obedient disposition, and a willingness toward all good things. Obedience makes a person submissive to the commandments of God and to the Divine will, and subjects the senses and the <term>animal powers<note>original: "beestelijcke craechten" — the basic instincts and sensory drives that humans share with animals, which Ruusbroec argues must be governed by the soul.</note></term> to the <term>higher reason<note>original: "auerste redene" — the superior faculty of the soul that perceives spiritual truth and directs the will toward God.</note></term>, so that the person lives rationally. It also makes the person obedient to the Holy Church and the Sacraments, and makes him submissive to his <term>prelates<note>original: "prelatē" — high-ranking church officials or spiritual superiors.</note></term>, their teachings, commandments, and counsels, and all the good customs of the Holy Church.

<margin>The effects of obedience.</margin>
It also makes the person ready and obedient among all people, in counsel and deed, and in every service, both physical and spiritual, according to each person’s need with <term>discretion<note>original: "bescheydentheyt" — a key virtue in mystical writing, meaning the ability to discern the right course of action and maintain moderation.</note></term>. It also drives away disobedience, which is a daughter of <term>pride<note>original: "hooverdicheyt" — considered the deadliest of sins because it mirrors Lucifer’s rebellion.</note></term>, and which must be shunned more than venom or poison. Obedience of will and of works, a- <note>The text ends mid-word; the catchword "ciert" suggests the full word is "verciert," meaning "adorns."</note>

<summary>This page defines obedience as a direct result of humility, explaining how it aligns the individual with God's will, the Church's authority, and the needs of others. It emphasizes the subordination of animal instincts to higher spiritual reason.</summary>
<keywords>obedience, humility, divine will, reason, Holy Church, prelates, discretion, pride</keywords>
