Whoever maintains this is loved by his superiors and by all people, and he is exalted and gifted by God with His graces, which are eternal.

## CHAPTER XVI.
### On the Renunciation of Self-Will

<margin>No one can deny his own will except the humble.</margin>
From this obedience comes the renunciation of one’s own will and <term>judgment<note>original: "goetdunckens" — an individual’s personal opinion or what seems good to them, as opposed to objective truth or divine command.</note></term>. For no one can renounce his own will, and go beyond himself into the will of another, except for the obedient person alone; for one can certainly perform all outward works and yet still remain <term>self-willed<note>original: "eygen willich" — stubborn or acting according to one’s own desires rather than out of obedience.</note></term>. The renunciation of self-will makes a person live without choosing this or that, or anything in doing or omitting that might be unlike the saints or their life or teaching; but on the contrary, it makes him always seek the glory of God, keep His commandments, and live according to the will of his superiors with right <term>discretion<note>original: "bescheydentheyt" — the spiritual virtue of discernment and moderation.</note></term>. Through the renunciation of self-will

The person who is thus has, in truth, put off the <term>old man<note>A reference to the Pauline concept (Ephesians 4:22-24) of the "old self" corrupted by desire versus the "new self" created in God's likeness.</note></term> and put on the new, who is entirely renewed according to God’s will. Of this Christ speaks: "Blessed are the poor in spirit"—that is, those who have renounced their own <margin>*Mat. 5*</margin> will—"for the kingdom of heaven belongs to them."

This causes a person to have mercy on themselves through righteous compassion.

Furthermore, compassion causes one to notice the wandering and error of mankind, and their neglect in pursuing or following after the eternal God; and also that they behold their great ingratitude for all the good that God has done for them, and for all the pains he has suffered for them. And [it shows] that they are coarse, ignorant, and unpracticed in virtue, yet clever and cunning in all wickedness and injustice. This observation creates within the good person a great compassion for the salvation of all people.

A person shall also notice with mercy the physical needs of their neighbor and their manifold natural suffering. When he sees their need—namely hunger, thirst, cold, nakedness, and

# CHAPTER XXII.

## Of Diligence or Industriousness

<margin>He who is generous is also diligent in practicing all virtues.</margin>
From this <term>generosity<note>original: "miltheyt" — also translated as liberality; the quality of being free in giving and open-hearted.</note></term> springs a supernatural diligence and industriousness for all virtues and for all propriety. No one can feel this diligence except a diligent, generous person. Diligence is nothing else but an internal, <term>restless drive<note>original: "onghedurighen drift" — here meaning a constant, unceasing movement or urge that does not allow the soul to remain stagnant.</note></term> toward all virtue and toward the likeness of Christ and all his saints. Through this virtue, man desires to commit his heart, his soul, body, and all that he is, has, and may acquire, to God’s praise and honor. This diligence makes man act according to reason with <term>discernment<note>original: "bescheydentheyt" — the ability to judge well and act with spiritual wisdom.</note></term>, and to work the virtues with soul and body in justice. By means of this natural industriousness, all the powers of the soul are opened to God and prepared for all virtues;

<margin>The effects of this spiritual industriousness.</margin>
the <term>conscience<note>original: "consciētie" — the inner sense of right and wrong, seen here as being "gladdened" when one is active in virtue.</note></term> is gladdened, the grace of God is increased, the virtues are practiced with delight and joy, and external works are adorned. He who has received this living industriousness from God has driven out the fifth mortal sin, namely <term>sloth of the mind<note>original: "traecheyt des gemoets" — traditionally known as acedia; a state of spiritual apathy or listlessness.</note></term>.

<summary>This page introduces the virtue of diligence (neersticheyt) as a direct result of generosity, describing it as a restless inner drive to imitate Christ. It explains how this industriousness activates the soul's powers and serves as the primary cure for the mortal sin of sloth.</summary>

<keywords>diligence, industriousness, generosity, Seven Deadly Sins, sloth, acedia, imitation of Christ, discernment, conscience</keywords>

## CHAPTER XXIII.

->Of Sobriety.<-

<margin>From where sobriety arises.</margin>
From this <term>diligence<note>original: "vlijticheyt" — the spiritual industriousness discussed in the previous chapter.</note></term> springs measure and sobriety, both inwardly and outwardly; for no one can rightly maintain the measure of sobriety except the one who is especially diligent and industrious in guarding their soul and body in righteousness. Sobriety separates the higher <term>powers<note>original: "crachten" — refers to the faculties of the soul, such as reason and will.</note></term> from the animal ones, <margin>The effect of this virtue.</margin> and preserves the person from immoderation and from excess. Sobriety does not wish to taste or know the things that are forbidden. The high, incomprehensible nature of God exalts all creatures in heaven and on earth; for all that the creature understands is <term>creaturely<note>original: "creature" — the author distinguishes between the finite nature of created things and the infinite nature of the Creator.</note></term>, and God is above all creatures.

# The Ornament

A person should take food and drink just as a sick person takes medicine: solely to maintain their strength in order to serve God. This is what is meant by <term>sobriety<note>In the medieval tradition of Jan van Ruusbroec, "sobriety" (Dutch: *soberheyt*) is broader than modern abstinence; it refers to a disciplined moderation in all physical and spiritual appetites.</note></term> of the body. Beyond this, a person must maintain a sense of <term>measure<note>Dutch: *maete*. This refers to the classical and Christian virtue of temperance—finding the "golden mean" in all things.</note></term> and decorum in words and in deeds, in silence and in speaking, in food and in drink, in acting and in refraining, according to the customs of the Holy Church and the example of the Saints. Because of this inward sobriety and measure of the spirit, a person retains the firmness and constancy of faith, purity of understanding, and a settled composure of reason necessary to understand the truth. This leads to an obedience to God’s will in all virtues, peace of heart, and an undisturbed conscience; and through this, he possesses constant peace in God and in himself.

<margin>The profit of outward sobriety.</margin>
Through the measure of sobriety regarding the physical senses from without, a person often maintains the health and well-being of their physical nature, as well as honor in their outward conduct and their reputation. Thus, he has peace within himself and with his <term>neighbor<note>original: "evenmaesten" — literally "even-highest" or "equal-mate," a common Middle Dutch term for fellow human beings.</note></term>, for he attracts and pleases all well-meaning people through his moderation and sobriety. By doing so, he drives away the <term>sixth deadly sin<note>The list and order of the Seven Deadly Sins varied slightly in medieval texts; here, gluttony is identified as the sixth.</note></term>, namely: excess and gluttony.

<margin>Matthew 5.</margin>
And of these Christ speaks: "Blessed are the peace-

<summary>This page defines the virtue of sobriety as a form of "medicine" for the body and soul, explaining how moderation in physical and spiritual matters leads to inner peace, health, and the avoidance of the deadly sin of gluttony.</summary>
<keywords>sobriety, moderation, temperance, deadly sins, gluttony, inner peace, health, neighborly love, Matthew 5</keywords>

From whence
purity
comes

spirit.

<margin>Purity of the heart: what it requires.</margin>

## THE XXV. CHAPTER.

### Of Justice

<margin>
How we must maintain justice in the purity of the heart.
</margin>
<margin>
Three enemies of the soul.
</margin>
<margin>
What are the weapons of these enemies.
</margin>
If we now wish to possess these virtues and drive away their opposites, then we must have the virtue of <term>justice<note>In this context, "justice" (gerechticheyt) refers to "righteousness" or "right-living"—giving both God and neighbor what is their due.</note></term>, and practice and maintain this in the purity of the heart until death. For we have three powerful enemies who tempt and assail us at all times, in all places, and in many ways. And if it happens that we make peace with or obey any of these three, then we are defeated, for they are united in all injustice and wickedness. These three enemies are the **world**, the **devil** <note>original: "dubel," a variant of "duivel"</note>, and our own **flesh**, which is nearest to us and often the most cunning and harmful because animal lust is the weapon with which these enemies fight us. Idleness and negligence toward virtue and toward the honor of God is a cause of the struggle; but the weakness of nature, lack of vigilance, and ignorance of the truth is the sword with which our enemies sometimes wound and defeat us, and

<summary>This page introduces the virtue of Justice as the essential guard for a pure heart, identifying the three traditional spiritual enemies—the world, the devil, and the flesh.</summary>
<keywords>Justice, three enemies, the world, the devil, the flesh, purity of heart, spiritual warfare, virtue</keywords>

->Of the Spiritual Espousals<-

and therefore we must make within ourselves a renunciation and division; and that lower part of ourselves <note>The "lower part" refers to the sensual or animal nature, which was believed to be in constant conflict with the higher, rational soul.</note> that is contrary to us in virtues and wishes to separate us from God, we must hate, persecute, and torment with penance and with strictness of life, so that it always remains suppressed and subject to reason; and so that justice and purity of heart always maintain the upper hand in all virtuous works.

And we shall gladly suffer all persecution and suffering that God allows to come upon us from all those who are contrary to virtue, for the honor of God and to obtain and possess justice in purity of heart.

For Christ speaks: "Blessed are those who suffer persecution for righteousness' sake, for the kingdom of heaven belongs to them." <note>A translation of Matthew 5:10.</note>

For where justice is maintained in suffering and in virtuous works, there is the <term>penny<note>original: "den penninck." A reference to the Parable of the Workers in the Vineyard (Matthew 20), where the penny represents the reward of eternal life given to those who serve God.</note></term> that weighs the same as the kingdom of God, and with it one buys eternal life.

With these virtues, man goes to God, to himself, and to his <term>fellow man<note>original: "even naesten," literally "equal-neighbor," emphasizing the shared humanity of one's peers.</note></term>, in good morals, in virtues, and in justice.

# CHAPTER XXVI.

### How one shall govern the kingdom of the soul.

<margin>How the soul must be a kingdom.</margin>
<margin>Who the King of this kingdom is.</margin>
Whoever wishes to obtain and keep these virtues must adorn, possess, and order his soul like a King does his kingdom. The King within the soul is the free will, which is free by nature and even freer by grace. This King shall be crowned with a crown called <term>charity<note>original: "charitate," meaning divine, self-giving love. In this context, it is the highest virtue that perfects the will.</note></term>. This crown and this kingdom one shall receive, possess, govern, and hold from the Emperor, who is Christ—the Lord and commander and King of Kings. This King—namely, this free will—shall dwell in the highest city of the kingdom, which is in the <term>desirous power<note>original: "begeerlijcke cracht." Medieval thinkers divided the soul into faculties; this specific power is where the soul's longing and love for God are situated.</note></term> of the soul. And he shall be adorned and clothed with a <term>divided garment<note>original: "doorsneden kleet." This refers to a "parti-colored" robe, common in medieval courtly fashion, where the two sides represent different attributes or virtues.</note></term>. The right side of the garment shall be the divine virtue called strength <note>original: "sterckheyt," referring to the cardinal virtue of Fortitude.</note>, so that he may be strong and powerful to overcome all hindrances and to walk in Heaven, in the Palace of the Supreme King; and so that he may bow his crowned head with love before the sovereign Lord with satisfied desire—for that is the proper work of love, through which one receives the crown and adorns it. And the kingdom is possessed and kept through this for eternity. The left side of the garment shall

<summary>Ruusbroec uses the allegory of a kingdom to describe the internal life, where the free will acts as a King crowned by charity and clothed in the virtue of strength.</summary>

<margin>Who the King’s counselors are.</margin>
This King shall choose counselors, the very wisest of his land: namely, the two divine virtues of knowledge and discernment <note>original: "wetenschap eũ bescheydentheyt." Knowledge here refers to a spiritual understanding of truth, while discernment is the practical ability to distinguish between good and evil.</note>, enlightened by the light of God’s grace. These shall dwell in a palace next to the King, called the <term>rational power<note>original: "redelijcke cracht." The faculty of reason or intellect.</note></term> of the soul. And they shall be clothed with a moral virtue called temperance <note>original: "maticheyt," meaning moderation or self-restraint.</note>, so that the King may always act and refrain in all things according to their counsel. By means of knowledge, one shall purify the conscience of all faults and adorn it with every virtue. And with discretion or discernment, one shall give and take, do and refrain, keep silent and speak, fast and eat, hear and answer, and do all things according to these two virtues, clothed in the moral virtue named temperance.

<margin>The judge of the spiritual kingdom.</margin>
This King, the free will, shall also appoint a judge in his kingdom; and that shall be justice, which is a divine virtue when it proceeds from love, and as such it is one of the highest moral virtues. This judge shall dwell in the mind <note>original: "gemoet," referring to the heart or inner disposition.</note> in the middle of the kingdom, in the <term>irascible power<note>original: "toornige cracht." This is the faculty of the soul used to resist evil and stand firm in justice; it is the "spirited" part of the soul that fights for what is right.</note></term>, and he shall be adorned with a moral virtue called wisdom; for justice cannot exist without this...

# The Ornament

...without this virtue, nothing can be perfect. This judge, <term>Justice<note>original: "gerechticheyt." And he shall appoint and depose, condemn and judge, kill and let live, lift up and cast down; and he shall arrange all things according to right, scourging and chastising, and renouncing all vice.

The common people of the kingdom are all the <term>powers of the soul<note>original: "crachten der zielen." In the medieval view, these include the "higher" faculties of memory, intellect, and will, as well as the "lower" sensory and emotional faculties.</note></term>. These shall be founded in humility and in divine fear, and they shall be subject to God in all virtues, each faculty according to its proper nature. He who has thus possessed, protected, and ordered the kingdom of the soul has "gone out" <note>original: "wtghegaen." This refers to the soul's active movement—leaving behind the "old life" of sin to meet God in the "spiritual espousals."</note> with love and with virtues toward God, toward himself, and toward his neighbor. These three ways of "going out"—by which a person leaves behind their old life through the aforementioned virtues—constitute the third of the four principal points of which Christ speaks:

<margin>Matthew 25.</margin>
"*Go out*" <note>original: "Exite, gaet wt." A reference to Matthew 25:6: "Behold, the bridegroom comes; go out to meet him."</note>.

then into the light of faith, and it is revealed and known that God is incomprehensible and unknowable. And where the soul inclines itself with desire toward the incomprehensibility of God, there it meets Christ and is filled with His gifts; and where it loves and rests above all gifts, above itself, and above all creatures, there it dwells in God and God in it. This is the manner in which we shall meet Christ in the highest stage of the <term>active life<note>original: "werckende leven." This refers to the first of three stages of the mystical path, focusing on outward moral actions and the cultivation of virtues.</note></term>. Now then, to speak briefly:

<margin>A short summary of what has been recounted in this book.</margin>
you have learned [to have] justice, charity, and humility as a foundation, and upon that you have built a dwelling—which are the virtues mentioned before—and if you have gone to meet Christ with faith, intention, and with love, then you dwell in God and God in you. And you have, in this first manner, fulfilled the words of Christ where He speaks thus: *See, the bridegroom comes, go out to meet him* <note>original: "Ecce sponsus venit"</note>, and you have possessed an active life.

<margin>Matthew 25.</margin>
And this is the first interpretation and unfolding that we desire to perform regarding these aforementioned words of Christ, our Bridegroom.

## The second book of the Spiritual Marriage, teaching how one shall begin an inward, elevated, and yearning life. Written by the enlightened visionary <term>John Ruusbroec<note>original: "Ioannes Ruysbrochius." A 14th-century Flemish mystic, known as "the Admirable," whose writings on the soul's journey toward God are foundational to Western mysticism.</note></term>.

# CHAPTER I.

### How one becomes supernaturally seeing in inward exercises.

<margin>three things necessary to see supernaturally.</margin>
Now then, Christ speaks in the first place: *See*. For whoever shall see supernaturally in inward exercises <note>Inward exercises refer to spiritual disciplines like meditation or prayer that focus on the internal state of the soul rather than external actions.</note>, three things are necessary for this. The first is the light of God's grace in a higher manner than any person may feel in the outwardly active life <note>The "active life" is the first stage of Ruusbroec's spiritual path, focused on moral living and good works.</note> without inward diligence. The second is a stripping away of strange images <note>In medieval mysticism, "images" refers to sensory memories or mental concepts that can distract the soul from the direct, "unimaged" presence of God.</note> and distractions of the heart, so that the person may be free, unimaged, and empty of all creatures. The third is a free turning of the will with a gathering of the physical and spiritual powers, unencumbered and loose from all disordered love <term>disordered love<gloss>original: "ongeordineerde liefde," referring to love directed toward created things for their own sake rather than toward God, which upsets the proper spiritual hierarchy.</gloss></term>, flowing into the unity of God and into the unity of thoughts, so that the rational creature in such a way might obtain and possess the high unity of God supernaturally. 

<margin>Why God has made all things.</margin>

<summary>Ruusbroec outlines three requirements for achieving supernatural spiritual vision: the light of grace, the removal of mental distractions (images), and the focused union of the will with God. ## THE III. CHAPTER,

### On the influence of God's graces in our Spirit.

<margin>
Matthew 25: through whom we become seeing.

From this unity where the spirit is united without intermediary <term>without intermediary: "sonder middel," a mystical term for a direct union with God that bypasses all symbols, thoughts, or secondary causes</term> with God, flows grace and all gifts. From this same unity where the spirit rests above itself in God, so speaks Christ, the eternal truth: "Behold, the Bridegroom comes, go out to meet Him." <note>original: "Siet den Bruydegom comt, gaet wt hem tegemoet," a central theme of this work, taken from the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25:6.</note> Christ, who is the light of eternal truth, speaks: "See," for because of Him we become seeing, because He is the light of the Father, and without Him there is no light in heaven or on earth. This "in-speaking" <note>original: "insprekẽ," referring to an internal divine communication or inspiration</note> of Christ within us is nothing other than a flowing-in of His light and His grace. This grace falls into us into the unity of our highest powers and our spirit, where the highest powers blossom forth visibly in all virtues through the power of grace, and return again into that same unity in the bond of love <term>love: "minnen," a Middle Dutch term often used by mystics to describe a deep, reciprocal, and divine love</term>.

## THE IV. CHAPTER.

### How we shall establish inwardness in unimagined freedom. <note>unimagined freedom: a state of the soul free from sensory images or mental distractions, allowing for direct contemplation of God</note>

<margin>Three things make the person spiritually seeing.</margin>
Now Christ speaks spiritually in the person who has turned inward; See. Three things (as I said before) make the person seeing in inward exercise <note>exercise: the regular practice of spiritual discipline or meditation</note>. The first is an illumination of God’s grace. The grace of God in the soul is like a candle in a lantern or in a glass vessel; for the candle heats, clarifies, and shines through the same; so also the grace of God heats, clarifies, and shines through the good person; and she reveals herself to the person who has her within, if he is inward in the observing of himself; and she also reveals herself to other people through him in virtues and in good examples. This insight of God’s grace stirs and moves the person internally from within suddenly; and this quick movement is the first thing that makes us seeing. From this quick movement comes the second thing on the person's part, which is a gathering of all powers from within and of <note>gathering of all powers: the process of "recollection," where the mind's faculties—memory, intellect, and will—are pulled away from external things to focus on the divine</note>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of Chapter 4, where the author describes the three elements necessary for spiritual sight. He uses the metaphor of a candle in a lantern to explain how God's grace illuminates, warms, and transforms the soul from the inside out.</summary>

<keywords>illumination, inwardness, grace, spiritual exercise, Jan van Ruusbroec, soul's faculties, metaphors of light</keywords>

<header>of the Spiritual Espousals.</header>

from without into the unity of the Spirit in the bond of love.

<margin>
How a 
person 
comes to 
a holy

The third thing is a freedom, such that the person may turn inward without images <note>images: mental pictures or sensory distractions that interfere with pure contemplation</note> and unhindered, as often as he wishes and remembers his God. This means that the person must be unconcerned with joy or sorrow, with gain or loss, with being exalted or oppressed, with delight or fear, with foreign cares, and with all attachment to created things. These three things make the person "seeing" in the inward exercise <note>exercise: the active practice of spiritual discipline</note>. If you have these three, then you have laid a foundation and a beginning of the inward exercise and of an inward life, in order to fulfill that first word that Christ speaks: "Behold" <note>original: "Ecce"</note>, see.

## CHAPTER VI.

### Of the second coming of Christ in the inward person

<margin>The dignity of the second coming of Christ in us.</margin>
The second manner of the inward coming of Christ is in higher nobility and likeness to Himself, and in greater gifts and clarity. This is an inflowing into the highest power of the soul with riches of divine gifts, which stabilize, illuminate, and make the spirit rich in many ways. This flowing of God into us demands an inflowing and a "re-flowering" <note>original: "wederbloeyen." Ruusbroec uses a play on words between "vloeyen" (flowing) and "bloeyen" (flowering/blooming), suggesting that as God flows into the soul, the soul must bloom back toward Him</note> with all the richness into the same ground from which that flowing comes. In this flowering, God shows great wonders; but He demands back from the soul all His gifts, multiplied beyond all that a creature is able to give back. This exercise and this state of being is nobler and more like God than the first; and by this, the three highest powers of the soul <note>In medieval psychology, these are typically the memory, the intellect, and the will</note> are adorned.

# CHAPTER VII.

## Of the third inward coming of Christ.

The third manner of the inward coming of our Lord is an inward stirring or touching in the unity of the spirit <note>Ruusbroec refers to the "unity of the spirit" as the essential ground of the soul, the point where the human spirit meets the Divine</note>, where the highest powers of the soul return inward from their outward activities <note>original: "wtvloyen," literally "out-flowing." The soul "flows out" to use its senses and "flows back" to rest in God</note>, and there is always a constant, unified inflowing through the bond of love and the natural unity of the spirit. This coming establishes the innermost and highest essence in an inward life, and by this, the unity of the spirit is adorned in many ways.

<margin>What Christ demands of us in each coming.</margin>
Now then, Christ demands in each coming a special "going out" <note>The "going out" (wtgaen) is the active response to God's "coming in." It refers to how a person puts their faith into practice</note> from ourselves, living our lives according to the manner of His coming. And therefore He speaks spiritually in our hearts in each coming:

> *Go out with your life and with exercises in the same manner that my grace, love, and gifts drive you.*

<margin>What evil comes from resisting the Holy Spirit.</margin>
According to the way that the Spirit of God hunts <note>original: "jaecht." This suggests a powerful, pursuing divine impulse</note>, drives, draws, flows into, and touches us, so must we go out and walk in inward exercises if we are to become perfect. But if we resist the Spirit of God through an inconsistent life <note>original: "ongelijckheyt van leven," meaning a life that does not mirror or "match" the divine likeness</note>, we lose that inward driving, and then the virtues will fail us.

<summary>This page describes the third and highest inward coming of Christ, which occurs in the "unity of the spirit," and emphasizes the necessity of responding to divine grace with active spiritual practice.</summary>
<keywords>third coming, unity of the spirit, inward life, grace, virtues, spiritual exercises, Jan van Ruusbroec</keywords>

And this happens in a shorter time than a moment, for God's work is swift; but whoever is to experience this must be seeing inwardly with the eyes of the understanding. ### CHAPTER X

#### How the virtues proceed from unity, and this concerns inwardness.

<margin>what inwardness is and how it works.</margin>
From this unity comes inwardness <note>original: "innicheyt." This term refers to a deep interiority or a state of being "turned inward" toward God’s presence.</note>, for no one can be inward unless he be gathered as one within himself. Inwardness means that the person is turned from within toward his own heart, so that he may thus understand and feel the working or the speaking of God within. Inwardness is a perceptible fire of love that the spirit of God has ignited and causes to burn. Inwardness burns, drives, and stokes <note>original: "stockt." In this context, it suggests the stirring or poking of a fire to make it burn more brightly.</note> the person from within / and he knows not where it comes from, or what has happened to him.

# THE XI CHAPTER

## Of sensible love.

From this inwardness comes a sensible love <note>original: "geuoelijcke liefde." This refers to an affective, emotional love that can be felt by the senses and the heart, rather than just an intellectual or spiritual concept.</note>, which permeates the human heart and the desiring powers <note>original: "begeerlijcke crachten." In medieval psychology, these are the faculties of the soul that "reach out" to desire and embrace an object.</note> of the soul.

No one may possess this desiring love with a sensible taste of the heart, unless they be inward in their mind <note>original: "ghemoet." This refers to one's inner disposition or state of soul.</note>.

<margin>
what sensible love is, and how it works.
</margin>
This sensible affection and love is a desiring taste and delight that one has for God, just as for an eternal good in which all goodness is enclosed. Sensible love gives leave to all creatures according to delight, but not according to necessity <note>This suggests that a person experiencing this spiritual love loses their emotional attachment to worldly things—they may use "creatures" (physical things) for survival, but they no longer crave them for pleasure.</note>. Inward love feels itself touched from within by eternal love <note>original: "minnen." Ruusbroec uses this specific word for the mutual, ecstatic love between God and the soul.</note>, which it must indeed practice. And finally, this inward love easily denies and scorns all things so that it may obtain the One it loves.

<summary>Ruusbroec describes "sensible love" as a profound, felt experience of God that permeates the heart and redirects one's desires away from worldly pleasures toward the eternal good.</summary>

## THE XII CHAPTER

->Of True Devotion<-

<margin>
What devotion is, and what it effects.
</margin>
Out of this sensible love <note>original: "geuoelijcke liefde" (OCR says "geboelijcke"). As established on the previous page, this refers to a love that can be physically and emotionally felt in the heart.</note> comes devotion toward God and toward His honor; for no one may have a desiring devotion in their heart except the person who bears sensible love and affection <note>original: "minne." Ruusbroec uses this word to describe the intense, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note> toward God. Devotion is as the fire of love and affection raises its flame of desire toward heaven. This devotion stirs and steers a person from without and from within to the service of God; it also makes body and soul flourish in honor and in dignity before God and before all people. This devotion is what God demands of us in all the service we shall do for Him. This devotion cleanses soul and body of all that may hinder and obstruct us; and finally, it points out and shows the right way to salvation <note>original: "saelicheyt." This term encompasses both eternal salvation and the state of being "blessed" or "happy" in God.</note>.

that he has granted us health, strength, and power, and at times sickness for our benefit; and that he has established outward necessity <note>original: "wtwendighen nootduft." What it is to praise God.
</margin>
Furthermore, to praise God means that man offers honor, reverence, and dignity to the divine majesty in all his life. To praise God is the very work of the Angels and of the saints in the <term>kingdom of heaven</term> <note>original: "hemelrijck." Worldly possessions or material wealth used for spiritual purposes.</note> and humble service, from without and from within. Those who do not praise God here shall remain eternally damned. To praise God is the most pleasant and joyful work of the loving heart, for that heart which is full of this praise desires that all creatures praise God. To praise God shall never come to an end, for that is our <term>salvation</term> <note>original: "saelicheyt." The state of eternal bliss and union with God.</note>. Rightly, then, shall we praise Him in eternity.

### THE FOURTEENTH CHAPTER.

On two kinds of sorrow that come
from gratitude.

From fervent thanking and praising there comes a two-fold woe <note>original: "wee." A deep, spiritual grief or distress.</note> of the heart and a pain in the desires. The first is because one falls short <note>original: "ontblijft." Meaning to remain behind or fail to meet a standard; the soul feels it can never thank God enough.</note> in thanking, in praising, in honoring, and in God’s service; the other is because one does not increase, as one desires, in love, in virtues, in faithfulness, and in perfect conduct, so that one might become worthy to thank, praise, and serve God as is fitting. <margin>these two sorrows are roots of all virtues</margin> These two woes and pains are both roots and fruits, the end and the beginning of all inward virtues. The inward pain and woe that spring from that falling short in virtues and in the praise of God is the highest work in this first mode of <term>inward exercise</term> <note>original: "inwendighe oeffeninghe." The disciplined practice of spiritual focus and virtuous living.</note>, and herewith it is perfectly accomplished.

# THE FIFTEENTH CHAPTER.

-> A parable from which one may <-
-> learn how to practice this first <-
-> coming. <-

<margin>A parable comparing natural fire to the fire of the Holy Spirit.</margin>

Now observe a parable from which you may recognize how this exercise must be. See, just as natural fire, through its heat and power, drives water up to the boil <note>original: "soepe." Literally "soup" or "seething," referring to the peak of the water's movement when heated.</note> (for this is its highest work), and then that water drips and falls back down to the same base, and is then driven up again to that same work of boiling through the power of the fire, in such a way that the water is always boiling and the fire is always driving: so too works the inward fire of the Holy Spirit. For it drives, stirs, and chases the heart and all the powers of the soul to the boil—that is, to praise and thank God in the manner I have described before. But we fall back again to the same base where the Spirit of God burns, in such a way that the fire of <term>love</term> <note>original: "minnen." A specific Middle Dutch term for the mystical, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note> is always ignited there, and the human heart always thanks and praises with words and with works, and always remains in <term>humility</term> <note>original: "nederhept." Literally "lowliness," the foundational virtue of the soul's recognition of its own smallness before God.</note>, so that one considers it a great thing that one must also God [and]...

<summary>Ruusbroec uses the metaphor of boiling water to explain the cyclical nature of spiritual exercise: the Holy Spirit heats the soul to a peak of praise, which then returns to a base of humility to be heated again. This "boiling" represents the active, fervent state of a soul in the first stage of mystical progress.</summary>
<keywords>parable, Holy Spirit, fire, boiling, humility, love, Jan van Ruusbroec, spiritual exercise, praise</keywords>

of the Spiritual Espousals.

...and would gladly do so, and considers what one does as very little.

## THE XVI. CHAPTER.

### Yet another parable of the same exercise.

<margin>A parable of the natural sun, compared to the eternal sun, God.</margin>
<margin>How our heart brings forth leaves and fruit.</margin>
This same thing may also be noted from this other parable: for just as when summer approaches and the sun rises high, it draws the moisture out of the earth through the roots, and causes the <term>rising sap</term> <note>original: "schelbloetsel oft t'sap." "Schelbloetsel" is an archaic term for the life-fluid or "blood" of the tree as it begins to flow in spring.</note> of the tree to enter the branches, and from this come leaves, flowers, and fruit. In such a manner also, when the eternal sun, Christ, rises and ascends in our hearts so that it becomes summer in the abundance of virtues, he radiates his light and heat into our desire, and draws the heart away from all the <term>multiplicity</term> <note>original: "menichvuldicheyt." A key concept in Flemish mysticism referring to the fragmented state of being distracted by many earthly concerns rather than being focused on the "One" (God).</note> of earthly things and creates unity and <term>inwardness</term> <note>original: "innichz" (innigheid). This refers to the interiority or "inward" turning of the soul toward God.</note>, and causes the heart to grow and leaf—that is, to bring forth leaves with inward love, to bloom with longing devotion, and to bring forth fruit with thanks and with praise, and to keep the fruit eternally in humble <term>sorrow</term> <note>original: "wee." A technical term for the "sweet woe" or the painful yet holy longing the soul feels because it is not yet fully united with God.</note>, which always springs from an <term>insufficiency</term> <note>original: "onblyven." Literally a "staying-short" or "non-remaining"; the feeling of falling short of the Divine ideal.</note>. Here ends the first manner of inward exercise of the four principal ones that adorn the lower part of man.

And if it so happens then that the <term>planets</term> <note>In medieval cosmology, the planets were believed to govern the "complexions" or natural states of all living things on earth.</note> which govern nature are well-disposed and tempered according to what the time of year requires, then the sun sends its radiance upon the earth and draws the moisture upward into the air, and from this comes dew and rain, and the fruit increases and is greatly multiplied. In like manner also, when Christ, that bright sun, is exalted in our hearts above all things, and when the desires of the bodily nature which are contrary to the spirit are well...

## CHAPTER XIX.

### Concerning spiritual drunkenness.

<margin>
What spiritual drunkenness effects.
</margin>
Out of this spiritual abundance comes <term>drunkenness</term> <note>original: "dronckenschap." This is a classic mystical metaphor (derived from the Song of Songs) describing a state where the soul is so overwhelmed by divine grace that it loses rational control, appearing "drunk" to the outside world.</note>. Spiritual drunkenness is when a person receives more delightful taste and abundance than their heart or their desire can wish for or contain. This spiritual drunkenness creates many strange behaviors in a person; for it causes some people to sing and praise God out of the fullness of joy, and it causes some to shed a multitude of tears

## CHAPTER XXII.

### On the third way of the spiritual
### coming of Christ.

<margin>When the heat is greatest.</margin>
When the sun of Heaven now reaches its highest point where it may go, namely in <term>Cancer</term> <note>original: "Cancro." The author uses the zodiac sign of the Crab (the Summer Solstice) to represent the peak of spiritual intensity.</note>—that is, in the Crab—because it can go no higher, but begins to go backwards; then the heat of the year is at its greatest, and then it draws the moisture upwards, and the earth becomes dry, and the fruit ripens most of all:

<margin>When Christ is exalted in the highest of our hearts.</margin>

The dog days, which bring with them many plagues and sicknesses, are a time when the season sometimes becomes so intemperate and so hot that in certain lands the herbs and trees wither and dry up; and in some waters many fish suffocate and die; and upon the earth some people die and languish. The sun alone is not the cause of this, for if it were, this plague would be common to all lands, waters, and people. Rather, the lack of tempering <note>original: "onghetempertheyt." # CHAPTER XXVII.

### A parable of the ant showing how man must conduct himself in this state.

<margin>The nature of an ant</margin>
I also want to present a short parable to these people, who walk in this restlessness of love <term>restlessness of love: "ongheduer der minnen," a state of spiritual tension where the soul fluctuates between intense longing and perceived absence</term>, so that they might pass through this way nobly and properly, and reach higher virtues. There is found a small creature among the animals which is called an ant, and it is strong and wise, and it dies very reluctantly <note>original: "sterft seer noode," meaning the ant is remarkably hardy and persistent in its survival</note>, and it loves to live in the community of a large company in hot, dry lands, and it works throughout the summer, gathering food and grain for the winter, and this it splits in two so that it does not rot, so that it may use it in the winter when nothing is
<margin>One should not choose to die quickly.</margin>
to be found; also, it does not make strange paths but always travels by one way; and when it has waited for its time, it can fly. So also should these people do; for they shall be strong in awaiting the coming of Christ, and wise against the illumination and promptings of the enemy; they shall also not choose

### CHAPTER XXIX

#### What the person must do who is thus resigned.

Now, the person who is thus resigned <term>resigned: the Dutch "ghelaeten" (resignation or abandonment) is a core mystical concept. It describes a state of "letting-be" where a person surrenders all self-will and personal desire to God's will, even in the midst of suffering.</term>, he gladly seeks out good people, and to these he laments and shows his misery, and he desires the help and the prayers of the holy church and of all good people. But here the person shall note with a humble heart that he has nothing of himself but deficiency; he shall speak with the holy Job in patience and resignation of himself:

> The Lord gave, and the Lord took away; as it has pleased the Lord, so it has happened; blessed be the name of the Lord. <note>original: "Godt gaf, Godt nam, alsoo alst dē Heere belieft heeft, soo ist geschiet, den naem des Heeren sy ghebenedijt." This is a paraphrase of Job 1:21.</note>

<margin>Job 2.</margin>
<margin>How those who are in distress and suffering should pray.</margin>
Also, he shall leave himself <note>meaning to "abandon" oneself or let go of self-interest</note> in all things, and speak and mean with his heart:

> Lord, I would as gladly be poor in all those things of which I am deprived as I would be rich, so far as it is Your will and for my salvation. Lord, not my will according to nature, but Your will—and my will according to the spirit—must be done; for I am Your own, and therefore I would as gladly be in hell as in Heaven for Your honor and glory.

See, in this way the person shall [rise] out of all his suffering and resignation...

<summary>Ruusbroec instructs the suffering soul to practice "gelatenheid" (resignation), using the biblical figure of Job as a model for accepting loss and aligning one's spiritual will with God's will, even to the point of accepting hell if it were for God's glory.</summary>

## CHAPTER XXXII:

**Concerning four kinds of spiritual fevers that can hinder a person in this manner.**

<margin>
Those who concern themselves with what does not concern them must often hear what does not please them.
</margin>

Sometimes, these people—who are filled with bad humors <term>humors: "humeuren," referring to the medieval medical theory that health was governed by four bodily fluids; here, Ruusbroec uses it to mean spiritual imbalances.</term>, that is, with a disordered inclination of the body and the strange comfort of created things—fall into four kinds of fevers. The first is called the "daily fever," and it consists of a multiplicity of heart <term>multiplicity of heart: "menichvuldicheyt van herten," a state of being distracted by many worldly things rather than focused on God.</term>; for those who fall into this state want to know and speak about everything, and they want to correct or judge everything. They often forget themselves, and they also carry many strange cares. Often they must hear what is against them and does not please them, and they are disturbed by small causes. Their thoughts are varied—now this, now that, now here, now there—they are like the wind. This is a daily fever because they are burdened and restless <note>original: "onledich." In this context, it refers to being "un-idle" or occupied with worldly distractions that prevent spiritual rest.</note> with it...

# CHAPTER XXXIV

### How a person must live so that they may be enlightened.

<margin>
Who has received the first coming of Christ.
</margin>
**T**he person who lives in moral virtues, according to God’s commandments and in righteous obedience, and who further practices inward virtues according to the manner and impulse of the Holy Spirit in all leanings and inspirations toward righteousness; and who seeks not themselves in time nor in eternity; and who can weigh and bear all darkness, heaviness, and all kinds of misery with righteous patience, and thanks God for everything, and offers themselves up in humble resignation <note>original: "ghelatentheyt." ## CHAPTER XXXIV

### How a person must live if they are to be enlightened.

<margin>Who has received the first coming of Christ.</margin>
The person who lives in moral virtues: according to the commandments of God and in righteous obedience; and who therein practices inward virtues according to the manner and movement of the Holy Spirit in all impulses and inspirations toward righteousness; and who does not <unclear>seek</unclear> himself in time nor in eternity; and who can <unclear>bear</unclear> and carry all darkness, heaviness, and all kinds of misery in righteous patience, and thanks God for everything, and offers himself up in humble surrender <note>original: "ghelatentheyt." # CHAPTER XXXVI.
## How the first river of this fountain adorns the memory.

<margin>The first river creates simplicity within the person.</margin>
The first river of grace which God causes to flow in this coming is a pure simplicity <note>original: "eenvuldicheyt." Literally "one-foldness." In mystical theology, this refers to a state where the soul is focused entirely on God, stripped of the "manifold" distractions of the world.</note> that shines in the spirit without distinction. This river takes its origin in the fountain in the unity of the spirit, and flows straight down and passes through all the powers of the soul, both the higher and the lower, and lifts them above all multiplicity <note>original: "menichvuldichept." The state of being fragmented or distracted by many earthly concerns; the opposite of spiritual simplicity.</note> and busyness, and creates in the person a simplicity and a roundness <note>original: "ront." The circle was viewed in the Middle Ages as the most perfect shape, symbolizing eternity and the soul's return to God. To be "round" is to be spiritually complete and centered.</note>, and gives him an internal bond in the unity of his spirit. Thus the person is elevated in his memory and released from strange distractions <note>original: "vrimde invallen." Literally "strange intrusions" or wandering thoughts that pull the mind away from prayer.</note> and from instabilities. But now Christ demands in this light an outgoing according to the manner of the light and of this coming; so then the person goes out

### THE XXXVII CHAPTER

### How the second river illuminates the understanding.

<margin>Where the second river comes from.</margin>
Through inward love, loving inclination, and divine faithfulness, the second river of the fullness of grace springs forth into the unity of the spirit; and that is a spiritual clarity that blooms and shines with distinction <note>original: "onderscheydt." This refers to the intellectual power of discernment, allowing the soul to see the specific nature of different virtues and truths.</note> in the understanding <note>original: "verstant." This is the faculty of the soul responsible for spiritual insight and intellectual comprehension.</note> in many ways, for that light shows and gives, in truth, distinction in all virtues. But this is not entirely placed within our power; for although we always have this light in our soul, God nevertheless causes it to be silent or to speak, and He may show it or hide it, give it or take it in time and moment; for that light is His, and therefore He works in this light as He wills, where He wills, for whom He wills, and what He wills. <margin>Who has no need of revelation.</margin> These people have no need of revelations, nor is it necessary that they be drawn above their senses; for their life, dwelling, way of life, and their very being is in the spirit, above sense and sensuality <note>original: "sinnelijcheyt." # The Adornment
<note>original: "Het cieraet." This refers to the second book of Ruusbroec's major work, "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage."</note>

[is] a Father always to a Son in eternity, and the Son [is Son] to a Father. ## CHAPTER XL.

### How the person who is established in love shall go out in four ways.

<margin>How one must go out.</margin>
Now then, the person who is thus established, dwelling in the bond of love <note>original: "minnen." Ruusbroec uses this Middle Dutch term to describe a reciprocal, mystical love between God and the soul.</note>, shall remain in the unity of the spirit, and with enlightened reason and with abundant charity <note>original: "charitate." This refers to the theological virtue of selfless love in action.</note> go out into heaven and earth, and perceive all things with clear discernment <note>original: "bescheydētheyt." The ability to distinguish between different spiritual states and moral choices.</note>, and give to all people out of genuine generosity <note>original: "miltheyt." A quality of spiritual liberality or open-handedness.</note> and from the divine richness. In four ways, then, this enlightened person is called upon to go out (not so much in a divine manner as he is inclined to do so through his own habitual practice of the virtues <note>original: "deughden." Ruusbroec emphasizes that at this stage, the practice of virtue has become a natural "habit" for the soul.</note>). Firstly, toward God and all the saints; secondly, toward all sinners and wicked people; thirdly, into purgatory <note>original: "vaghevier." In medieval theology, the place of purification for souls before entering heaven.</note> to the grieving souls; and lastly, toward himself and toward all good people.

## CHAPTER XLII.

### Concerning the second outgoing toward all sinful people.

<margin>God desires to be entreated.</margin>
This **enlightened person** <term>enlightened person: in Ruusbroec's mystical theology, this refers to a soul that has been transformed by divine grace and now sees with spiritual clarity.</term> will sometimes descend with great **compassion** <note>original: "compassie"</note> and with generous mercy toward all sinners, and will offer these to God with inward devotion and with powerful prayer. He will remind God of all the goodness that He is, all that He is capable of, and all that He has promised and done for us, just as if He had forgotten it; for God desires to be entreated, and love does not allow itself to be deprived of its prayer and desire. Nevertheless, this soul does not wish to be stubborn or self-willed, but it commits everything to the rich goodness and generosity of God,

<margin>The enlightened person prays for everyone.</margin>
for he knows well that God loves without measure, and in this he remains most at peace. And because this person bears a **common love** <term>common love: translated from "gemeyn minne," this is a central concept for Ruusbroec, describing a love that is not private or exclusionary but flows out universally to all creatures, mimicking God's own love.</term>, he prays and desires that God let His love and mercy flow into heathens, Jews, and into all unbelieving people, so that He may be loved, known, and believed in the **kingdom of heaven** <note>original: "hemelrijck"</note>, and that our glory, joy, and peace may be increased

<meta>The text breaks mid-sentence as the soul's prayer for the expansion of divine peace continues onto the next page.</meta>

<summary>Ruusbroec describes the "second outgoing" of the enlightened soul, which involves descending from divine contemplation to pray with intense compassion for the salvation of all humanity. The soul acts as an intercessor, reminding God of His mercy and expressing a "common love" that encompasses believers and non-believers alike.</summary>

# CHAPTER XLIII.

## Concerning the third outgoing toward our friends in purgatory.

<margin>In what manner the **enlightened person** <term>enlightened person: a soul that has reached a high level of spiritual clarity through God's grace.</term> prays for the souls in purgatory.</margin>
Sometimes this person will also behold his friends in **purgatory** <note>original: "vaegheuier"; in medieval theology, a place of temporary suffering where souls are purified before entering heaven.</note> and observe their misery, longing, and their heavy pain. Then he will adore and invoke the grace, mercy, and generosity of God, and show Him their good will, their great misery, and their longing for the rich goods of God, and remind God that they died in **love** <note>original: "minne"; often used by Ruusbroec to describe mystical, divine love.</note>, and that all their trust rested in His passion and mercy.

<margin>One is sometimes moved by the Holy Spirit to pray for a specific cause.</margin>
Now we shall note that it might sometimes happen that this enlightened person may occasionally be moved in a special way by the Spirit of God to pray for a specific cause—for a sinner, or for a soul in purgatory, or for some other spiritual benefit—so that he clearly perceives that this does not come from his own will, nor from nature, but rather that it is an illumination of the **Holy Spirit**; and then this person becomes so **inward** <note>original: "innich"; a state of intense, focused devotion and interiority.</note>

### CHAPTER XLV.

->How one shall recognize those who fall short<-
->in common love.<-

<margin>How one may know oneself and be known by others</margin>
Now one finds certain people who are very subtle in words and skillful at demonstrating high things, yet they do not taste the enlightened ways, nor **common love** <term>common love: "gemeyn minne," a core concept in Ruusbroec's theology describing a love that is both focused on God and flows out universally to all people.</term> in generosity. But so that these people might learn to know themselves and be known by others, I will show them to you with three points. From the first, they may know themselves; and from the other two, anyone who is discerning may recognize them. 

Firstly, then: where an **enlightened person** <term>enlightened person: a soul that has reached a state of spiritual clarity and is guided by divine light.</term> is simple, steadfast, and without manifold distraction <note>original: "menichvuldich bemerc"; a state of being mentally cluttered with many observations rather than focused on divine unity.</note> by means of a divine light, these people are, on the contrary, manifold, unsteadfast, and full of observations and study; they taste no inward unity, nor unimaginable rest <note>original: "onverbeelde gerustheyt"; a peace that exists beyond mental images or conceptual thought.</note>.

and from this they may recognize themselves. Secondly: where the **enlightened person** has an infused wisdom from God in which he recognizes the truth with discernment without effort, this other person has subtle notions which he imagines and diligently observes with cleverness; but he is neither rich in his "ground" <note>original: "gront rijck"; referring to the "ground of the soul," the deepest, most essential part of the human spirit where it meets God.</note> nor generous in bringing forth teaching. His teaching is filled with many strange and subtle things, and it disturbs, hinders, and unquiets inward-looking people, for it neither leads nor points toward unity <note>original: "eenicheyt"; the state of being gathered into God.</note>, but instead teaches clever observation of multiplicity <note>original: "menichvuldicheyt"; the state of being distracted by many external or intellectual details.</note>. 

<margin>Those who are not upright are stubborn in preserving their own teaching.</margin>
These people are stubborn in defending their own teaching and opinions, even if another view were just as good as theirs; and they are unpracticed and reckless in all virtues, and spiritually proud in their whole being. 

Thirdly: whereas the enlightened, loving person—as you have heard—flows universally in charity <note>original: "ghemeyn in charitate"; the "common love" that flows out to all of creation without distinction.</note> toward both heaven and earth, these people are, on the contrary, singular and self-centered in all their dealings. ### CHAPTER XLVI.

->How Christ was, is, and remains<-
->a universal lover without end<-

<margin>What we must do to reach the highest degree of love.</margin>
**I**n order that we might possess and desire this universal way <term>universal way: original "ghemeyne wijsse"; a state of soul that is perfectly balanced, flowing out to all people in love while remaining gathered in God.</term> above all the ways of which we have spoken—because it is the highest—we will take Christ as an example, who was, is, and remains most universal <note>original: "ghemeyn"; Christ is "common" or "universal" because His love excludes no one.</note> in eternity; for he was sent universally to the earth for the benefit of all people who...

    *   *Text:* "soul/ mother and disciple/ mantle and coat/ he ate/ drank for our sake/ he lived and died for our sake/ his pain/ his pains and miseries were his own / but the profit that has come from them is shared/ and the glory of his merits shall eternally be shared."
    *   *Translation:* Christ's relationship to all (soul, mother, disciple) and his physical needs (clothing, food) were all for the benefit of others. His suffering was personal, but the spiritual profit is "common" (shared).

    *   *Text:* "Now has Christ his treasure and his rents left on earth/ these are the seven Sacraments and that outward good of the Holy Church/ that he with his death has labored for and that should be shared/ and his servants who lived from it should be shared/ and all those who from alms live and are in spiritual state/ should be shared/ at least in prayer and also all spiritual folk who in cloisters and cells are.     *   *Translation:* Christ left spiritual "treasure" and "rents" (the Sacraments and church property). Clergy and religious should be "common" (available to all, living without private interest). This was the model of the early Church leaders who were martyrs.

### CHAPTER LXI.

### On the unity of the divine nature in the trinity of persons, which is the primary cause of all spiritual activity.

<margin>The unity of the divine nature is fruitful.</margin>
The high, super-essential <note>original: 'ouerwesentlijcke'; this term describes God's existence as being "beyond essence" or above all created ways of being</note> unity of the divine nature, where the Father and the Son possess their nature in the unity of the Holy Spirit, is beyond the grasp and understanding of all our powers in the naked essence of our spirit. In that high silence, God transcends all creatures in created light. This high unity of the divine nature is living and fruitful; for in this same unity, the Eternal Word <note>The "Eternal Word" refers to the second person of the Trinity, Jesus Christ, as the Logos</note> is born of the Father without ceasing. And by means of this birth, the Father knows the Son and all things, and the Son knows the Father

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

ward dwelling and a kingdom of God and of His saints, filled with glory and with eternal joy. And because this heaven is an eternal and unmixed clarity, there is here neither time nor hour, nor movement, nor ever any change, for it is steady and unshakeable above all

things. The next sphere of the fiery Heaven is called the first movement <note>original: 'eerste beroeringhe'; this refers to the *Primum Mobile*, the outermost moving sphere in the medieval model of the universe which was believed to drive the motion of all other heavens.</note>. From here all motion arises out of the highest Heaven through the power of God; from this movement, the firmament and all the planets revolve, and from this, all creatures grow and live. ### CHAPTER LVI.

#### Of the fruitful works of the 
#### spirit which are eternal.

<margin>Why the highest powers do not remain in the unity of the spirit.</margin>
NOW is our spirit and this love <note>The original Dutch *minne* refers to a high, reciprocal, mystical love.</note> living and fruitful in virtues; and for this reason, the powers cannot remain and endure in the unity of the spirit. For the incomprehensible clarity <note>original: *claerheyt*; refers to the divine light or radiance.</note> of God and His bottomless love hold themselves above the spirit, and touch the loving power; and the spirit falls back into its works with a higher and more inward desire than before. And the more inward and noble it is, the more quickly it annihilates itself and works itself out in love, and falls again into a new work; and this is a Heavenly life.

<margin>The spirit that intends to devour God is itself devoured.</margin>
A greedy spirit always desires to eat and devour God, but it remains itself devoured in God’s touch, and fails <note>The Dutch *ontblijft* implies the spirit ceases its own independent activity.</note> in all its works and becomes love itself above all works. For in the unity of the spirit is a gathering <note>The OCR shows "een ghe", likely representing *een gaderinge* or *eenicheyt*.</note> of the highest powers; and here grace and love are essentially above all works, for this is the source of charity <note>original: *charitaten*, from the Latin *caritas*, meaning selfless Christian love.</note> and all virtues. Here is an eternal flowing-out in charity.

# The Ornament
<note>original: *Het cieraet*; referring to the title of the work, *The Ornament of the Spiritual Marriage*.</note>

<margin>What still remains to be noted.</margin>
We shall proceed with exercise according to the way that God, from within, in His coming, inflames <note>original: *ontfunct*; to kindle or set on fire, used here to describe the Holy Spirit's effect on the soul.</note>, teaches, and moves us. It behooves us to notice the fourth and final point: the meeting with Christ, our Bridegroom. For all our inward spiritual seeing in grace and in glory, and all our virtuous "going out" <note>term: *going out* (Dutch: *uutgaen*); in mystical theology, this refers to the soul performing active works of virtue and charity in the world, as opposed to "turning inward" for contemplation.</note> in whatever exercise it may be, is all for the sake of a meeting and a union in Christ our Bridegroom.

<margin>Christ is our eternal rest.</margin>
For He is our eternal rest, the end and the reward of all our labor. You know well that every meeting happens in a gathering of two persons who come from different places, and who are opposite and distinct from one another.

<margin>From where Christ comes to us, and from where we come to Him.</margin>
Now, Christ comes from above as a Lord and a generous giver who has power over all; and we come from below as poor servants, capable of nothing by ourselves but in need of everything. Christ comes into us from the inside outwards, and we come to Him from the outside inwards. Therefore, a spiritual meeting must take place here. This coming and this meeting of ourselves and of Christ occurs in two ways: that is, with a means and without a means <note>term: *with and without a means*; "With a means" refers to reaching God through intermediaries like sacraments, virtues, or creation; "without a means" refers to the highest state of mystical union where the soul encounters God's essence directly.</note>.

<meta>He</meta>

<summary>Ruusbroec introduces the fourth stage of the spiritual life: the meeting between the soul and Christ. He describes this encounter as a union of opposites—the powerful Lord from above and the needy servant from below—occurring both through spiritual practices (means) and direct mystical experience (without means).</summary>

# CHAPTER LVIII.

## Concerning the essential meeting with God in
## bare nature, which is without means.
<note>term: *without means* (Dutch: *sonder middel*); in mystical theology, this refers to a direct contact between the soul and God that occurs without the help of images, thoughts, or intermediaries like the senses.</note>

How the
unity of
our spirit
maintains

two ways.

For that being and that life which we are in God—within our eternal image—and which we have and are in ourselves according to our essential being, is without means and uncreated. <note>original: *ongheschepden*; uncreated. Ruusbroec refers to the "eternal image" of the human soul that exists in the mind of God from all eternity, distinct from the created physical person.</note> Therefore, the spirit, according to its innermost and highest part, receives in bare nature the impression of its eternal image and divine clarity without ceasing.

an eter-

God.

For where He comes, there He is; and where He is, there He comes; and where He never was, there He never...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the spirit’s essential unity with God. Ruusbroec explains that this unity is a natural foundation common to all humans, rather than a state of grace or holiness achieved through effort.</meta>

# The Spiritual Espousals

<margin>The dignity of the essential unity of our spirit</margin>
[This unity] does not rest upon itself, but it remains in God, it flows out of God, it hangs in God, and it returns again into God as into its eternal cause; and it never separated from God, nor ever shall it do so in this manner. For this unity is in us in bare nature; <note>term: *bare nature* (Dutch: *blooter naturen*); refers to the soul's fundamental state of being as created by God, prior to any actions, merits, or the addition of supernatural grace.</note> and if the creature were separated from God in this manner, it would fall into a pure nothingness. And this unity is above time and place, and works always without ceasing after the manner of God, except for the impression of its eternal image, which it receives passively <note>original: *lydende*; literally "suffering" or "enduring," used here in the philosophical sense of being the recipient of an action rather than the performer of one.</note> and not actively, like that which is like God and yet in itself entirely a creature.

<margin>The natural unity of our spirit with God does not make us holy but is indeed the first cause of all holiness.</margin>
This is the nobility that we have by nature in the essential unity of our spirit, where it is naturally united with God. This does not make us holy nor blessed, for all people have this within them, the good as well as the evil; but it is indeed the first cause of all holiness and blessedness. And this is that meeting and the union of God and our spirit in bare nature.

<sig>K iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>Ruusbroec defines the "essential unity" of the spirit as a natural, permanent bond with God that prevents the soul from falling into nothingness. He clarifies that while this unity is the foundation for all holiness, it is a natural gift shared by all humans, not a sign of personal sanctity.</summary>

<keywords>essential unity, bare nature, imago Dei, Jan van Ruusbroec, mysticism, creation, nobility of the soul, passive reception</keywords>

# THE LXI. CHAPTER.

### How the grace of God is necessary, which makes us like Him, and leads us into God without mediation.

<margin>Wherein all holiness lies.</margin>
NOW all holiness and blessedness depends upon this: that the spirit, through likeness and the mediation <note>original: *middel*; here meaning an intermediary or a bridge that connects two things.</note> of grace or of glory, be led into rest in the <term>essential unity <note>original: *wesentlijcke eenicheyt*; the deepest core of the soul where it is united with God's own being.</note></term>; for the grace of God is the way that we all must travel together, if we are to come into that bare being where God gives Himself without mediation in all His richness.

<margin>Why the sinners and the damned are in darkness.</margin>
And for this reason, the sinners and the damned spirits are in darkness, because they lack the grace of God which should enlighten, lead, and point them toward the <term>fruition-giving unity <note>original: *ghebruyckelijcke eenicheyt*; a state of "enjoying" or "possessing" God in a blissful, contemplative union.</note></term>.

<margin>Why the damned do not wish to perish.</margin>
Nevertheless, the essential being of the spirit is so noble that the damned neither want nor are able to perish <note>original: *te niet gaen*; literally "to go to nothing" or "to be annihilated."</note>, but sin creates a barrier, darkness, and such great unlikeness between the <term>powers <note>original: *crachten*; refers to the soul's faculties such as will, memory, and intellect.</note></term> and the being where God lives, that the spirit cannot find union in its own being—which is nonetheless its own and its eternal rest. And sin causes this; for he who lives without sin

<summary>Ruusbroec explains that divine grace is the essential bridge that transforms the soul to be like God, allowing it to enter into a direct, blissful union with Him. He notes that even the damned retain a noble essence that cannot be annihilated, but their sin creates an insurmountable barrier of darkness that prevents them from finding rest in God.</summary>

## CHAPTER LXII.
### Of the visitation of God and our spirit in unity and in likeness.

<margin>
What

Now God beholds the dwelling and the rest which He has made with us and in us—that is, <term>unity <note>original: *eenicheyt*; in Ruusbroec’s thought, this refers to the deepest essence of the soul where it is united with God beyond all activity.</note></term> and <term>likeness <note>original: *ghelijckheyt*; the state of being "like" God through grace and virtuous living.</note></term>—and He desires to visit this unity without ceasing through the new coming of His higher birth. And with rich inflows of His bottomless love—for He desires to dwell in delight within loving spirits—He also desires to visit the likeness of our spirit with rich gifts, so that we may become more like [Him] and clearer in virtues. Now Christ desires that we dwell and remain rich with Him in the <term>essential unity <note>original: *weselijcke eenicheyt*; the soul's ontological oneness with God.</note></term> of our spirit, above all creaturely works and above all virtues; and that we remain rich and filled with virtues and heavenly gifts <term>effectively <note>original: *werckelijck*; also translated as "actively," referring to the life of good works and spiritual practice.</note></term> in that same unity. And He desires that we visit that unity and that likeness without ceasing in every work that we perform; for in every <term>new now <note>original: *nieuwen nu*; a famous mystical concept in which God is not a historical event but is eternally being born in the soul in the present moment.</note></term> God is born within us, and toward this high birth the Holy Spirit flows with all His gifts. Now we shall meet the gifts of God with likeness, and the high birth in unity.

<summary>Ruusbroec explains that God constantly visits the soul in both its quiet essence (unity) and its active life (likeness), emphasizing that Christ is born anew within the believer in every "new now" through the Holy Spirit.</summary>

## CHAPTER LXIII.
### How we shall meet God in all works.

<margin>
The virtue and power of the simple intention
</margin>
Now understand how we shall meet God in every work and increase in greater <term>likeness <note>original: *ghelijckhept*; refers to the soul's active conformity to God through virtue.</note></term>, and more nobly possess the <term>fruitive unity <note>original: *gebruyckelijcke eenicheyt*; a central term for Ruusbroec meaning the "enjoyable" or "experiential" oneness with God where the soul finds its rest.</note></term>. Every good work, however small it may be, that is offered to God with love and with a right and <term>simple intention <note>original: *eenvuldighe meyninghe*; the focused directing of one’s will toward God alone, without distraction or selfish motive.</note></term>, merits greater likeness and eternal life in God. The simple intention draws the scattered powers [of the soul] together into unity of spirit and joins the spirit with God. The simple intention is the end and the beginning and the ornament of all virtues. This simple intention offers praise and honor to God and all virtues; and it leads and passes through itself, the heavens, and all things, and it finds God in the <gloss>everlasting</gloss> ground of itself. The intention is simple when it intends nothing but God, and [regards] all things in order toward God. This simple intention drives away hypocrisy and <term>duplicity <note>original: *tweevuldicheyt*; literally "two-foldness," the opposite of "simplicity" or "singleness" of heart.</note></term>, and a person should maintain and practice it in all his works above all things; for it keeps God present to the person, clear in the understanding, diligent in virtues, and free from strange fears.

<summary>Ruusbroec explains that any good deed, no matter how small, brings the soul closer to God if performed with a "simple intention." This single-minded focus on God unifies the soul's powers and allows it to find God within its own deepest ground.</summary>
<keywords>simple intention, unity, likeness, virtues, Jan van Ruusbroec, spiritual practice, duplicity, ground of the soul</keywords>

## CHAPTER LXIV.

### An order of all virtues through the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit

<margin>
When
man
is
like
God
</margin>
Observe now the order and steps of all virtue and holiness, how we shall meet God in likeness, so that we may rest with Him in unity. When man lives in the fear of God, in moral virtues and in outward exercises, and is obedient to the <term>Holy Church <note>original: *H. kercken*</note></term> and the commandments of God, and is ready for all good things in simple intention, then he is like God through faithfulness and the harmony of his will in acting and refraining according to the will of God; and he rests

## THE LXVII CHAPTER

### Of the first step of the gift of understanding, through which our spirit is established in unity.

And for this reason, God gives him the sixth gift, which is the **spirit of understanding**. We have previously compared this gift to a fountain of three rivers; for it establishes our spirit in <term>unity <note>original: *eenicheydt*; in Flemish mysticism, this refers to the "simple" or "one-fold" state of the soul where its various powers (will, intellect, memory) are gathered together and focused entirely on God.</note></term>, it reveals the truth, and it creates a wide <term>love in commonality <note>original: *minne in ghemeynheydt*; a key Ruusbroecian concept where the soul’s love is not just private, but becomes universal, flowing out to all people and all things, mirroring God's own "common" or "universal" love.</note></term>. This gift is also much like the radiance of the **Sun**; for the Sun fills the air with its radiance with a simple clarity, and it illuminates all forms and shows the distinction of all colors, thereby making known its own power, and how its heat is common throughout all the world for the benefit of all fruitfulness.

In the same way, the first insight of this gift creates <term>simplicity <note>original: *eenvuldicheyt*; literally "one-foldness," the opposite of being "manifold" or distracted by many worldly things.</note></term> in the spirit, and this simplicity is shone through with a special clarity, just like the air of the Heavens with the light of the Sun. For the <term>grace of God <note>original: *gratie Godts*</note></term>, which is the foundation of all gifts, shines purely in her essence...

<vocab>gift of understanding, unity, truth, love, Sun, clarity, grace of God, foundation</vocab>

<summary>Ruusbroec introduces the Gift of Understanding, comparing its spiritual effects to the Sun illuminating the air. This gift gathers the soul into a state of unity and universal love, preparing it for deeper enlightenment.</summary>
<keywords>Gift of Understanding, unity, simplicity, divine sunlight, universal love, commonality, grace, Jan van Ruusbroec</keywords>
