as a <term>simple light <note>original: *eenvuldich licht*; meaning a light that is undivided and pure, stripping away the "manifold" distractions of the world.</note></term> in our <term>possible understanding <note>original: *moghelijc verstant*; a philosophical term referring to the intellect's capacity to receive and be shaped by divine truth.</note></term>. And by means of this simple light, our spirit is established, simplified, and clarified, full of grace and divine gifts. And here, the spirit is **like God** by means of grace and divine love. 

And because it is like God, and intends and loves God simply above all gifts, it does not allow itself to be satisfied with mere likeness, nor with any created clarity. For it has a fundamental inclination—both natural and supernatural—toward an <term>abyssal being <note>original: *afgrondich wesen*; Ruusbroec's signature term for the "bottomless" or "unfathomable" essence of God.</note></term> from which it first flowed out. And the unity of the divine being has an eternal drawing, pulling all likeness into its own oneness. 

And for this reason, the spirit sinks away from itself <term>fruatively <note>original: *gebruyckelijc*; related to the concept of *fruitio*, or the joyful, restful enjoyment of God's presence as opposed to the "work" of virtue.</note></term>, and flows into God as into its eternal rest. For the grace of God holds itself to God just as the ray of light holds to the **Sun**; and it is a means and a way that leads us to God. Therefore, it shines simply within us and makes us closer to God—that is, it makes us like Him. And that likeness sinks away from itself at every hour; it dies in God and becomes one with God, and remains one. For love causes us to become one with God, and to remain and dwell in God. For the higher the love, the greater the rest; and the greater the rest, the more inward the love; for the one lives in the other. For he who does not love does not rest, and <margin>Why some believe they do not love God.</margin> he who does not rest does not love. Nevertheless, it seems to such a good person that he does not love, nor does he rest in God. This very thought comes from love: because he desires to love more than he is able, it seems to him that he falls short. And in this work, he tastes love and rest; for no one can understand how one actively loves and fruatively rests except the <term>resigned <note>original: *ghelaeten*; describing a state of "Gelassenheit" or yielding one's will entirely to God.</note></term>, empty, and clarified person. and for this reason, if man is to taste God
so must he love / and
if he wishes to love, so may he taste.

what God is / and for this reason

God above ourselves through

<term>universal man <note>original: *ghemeynen mensch*; Ruusbroec uses "common" or "universal" to describe a person who is perfectly balanced, open to all, and able to transition between action and contemplation without losing their center in God.</note></term> in unity becomes
steadfast.

<summary>Ruusbroec explains that the soul's balance of action and rest mirrors God's own nature, which is simultaneously the stillness of Divine Unity and the activity of the Trinity. He concludes that true spiritual understanding requires a person to be "universal," holding both virtue and divine rest together.</summary>

## CHAPTER LXVIII.
### Of the second step of the gift of understanding.

<margin>
A beautiful comparison
</margin>
Just as the air is shone through with the clarity of the sun, and so the beauty and richness of all the world is revealed, and the eyes of man are clarified, and he is gladdened in the manifold variety of **colors**; so it happens also when we are simple in ourselves, and our <term>potential intellect <note>original: *mogelijck verſtāt*; in scholastic philosophy, the "intellectus possibilis" is the capacity of the human mind to receive and process spiritual truths.</note></term> is clarified and shone through with the spirit of understanding. Then we may recognize the high **attributes** that are in God, which are the cause of all His overflowing works.

<margin>
When we may understand God's attributes
</margin>
Although all people may understand the works [of God], and God by means of His outward works, yet no one can feelingly or properly understand the nature of God’s works according to the manner of their <term>ground <note>original: *gronts*; a vital term in Middle Dutch mysticism referring to the deepest, essential core of both God and the human soul where the two meet.</note></term>, except through this gift. For it teaches us to contemplate and recognize our own nobility, and it gives us discernment in virtues and in all exercises—how we shall live without straying from the **eternal truth**. And those whom it enlightens may walk in the spirit.

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>Ruusbroec compares the gift of understanding to sunlight, explaining how it moves the soul beyond a general awareness of God's works to a specific, enlightened perception of His divine attributes and the soul's own nobility.</summary>

# CHAPTER LXIX.
## Of the third step of this gift.

<margin>The gift of understanding establishes in us common love.</margin>
In the measure that the air is clarified by the brightness of the sun / accordingly the heat becomes great and universal in fruitfulness. So also it is that our reason and understanding are thus enlightened to recognize with discernment the Divine truth / so the will / that is the loving power, is heated in a rich outflowing with faithfulness and love into <term>commonness <note>original: *gemeyntheyt*; the state of being "universal" or "common," where a person’s love is not private or selfish but flows out to all creatures.</note></term> / for this establishes in us a wide, common love through knowledge of the truth that we obtain through her clarity / for the simplest [souls] are the most settled and best at rest in themselves / and they are most deeply sunken in God / clearest in the understanding / most manifold in good works / and

<margin>The more we are like God, the more we are united with Him.</margin>
And the more we are like God in these three [ways], the more we are united with Him; and for this reason, we shall remain simple in our <term>ground <note>original: *grondt*; the innermost essence of the soul where it meets God.</note></term>, observing all things with enlightened reason, and flowing through all things with <term>common love <note>original: *gemeyne minne*; a central concept for Ruusbroec, describing a love that is both contemplative and active, reaching out to all of creation.</note></term>, just like the sun in the heavens, which in itself remains simple and unchanging, and yet pours out its clarity and heat universally over all the world.

<margin>How we shall obtain humility.</margin>
Now we must explain how we shall walk with enlightened reason in common love. The Father is the principle of all <term>Godhead <note>original: *Godtheyt*; the divine nature or essence.</note></term> according to essence and persons; therefore we shall bow down in spirit with humble reverence before the majesty of the Father, and so we possess <term>humility <note>original: *ootmoedicheyt*</note></term>, which is the foundation of all virtues.

<margin>How we are spiritually exalted.</margin>
We shall worship Him inwardly—that is, offer honor and spiritual dignity to the power of the Father—and so we are spiritually exalted, for He in His power creates and sustains all things from nothing. We shall give praise and thanks and eternal service to the faithfulness and love of God

Why the first mode is called **empty**.

Sometimes the inward person turns himself longingly and actively <note>original: *werckelijck*; referring to the active engagement of the will and spiritual effort.</note> toward God, so that he may offer God honor and dignity, and sacrifice and consume himself and all that he is capable of in the <term>love <note>original: *minne*; a specific Middle Dutch term for the mystical, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note></term> of God; and here he meets God through an **intermediary** <note>original: *met middel*; meaning that the soul reaches God through a specific channel or divine gift, rather than in a direct, unmediated union.</note>, which is the gift of **tasting wisdom** <note>original: *smaeckender wijsheyt*; this translates the Latin *sapientia*, which shares a root with *sapere* (to taste). This wisdom is a ground and origin of all virtues, and it establishes and moves every good person in virtues according to the measure of his love; and sometimes it so greatly stirs and ignites the inward person in love, that all the gifts of God and all that God may give besides Himself are too small for him, and cannot satisfy him, but rather increase his **restlessness** <note>original: *ongeduer*; a "holy unrest" or spiritual impatience where the soul’s desire for God becomes so intense it feels like a painful lack.</note>. Its followers remain in a still sitting, without internal or external exercise, in a <term>vacancy <note>original: *ledicheyt*; a state of being "empty" or "idle." And in this natural rest one cannot <note>The OCR reads *binden* (bind), but the marginal note and context suggest *vinden* (find). Ruusbroec argues that God cannot be "found" or "captured" in a purely psychological state of relaxation.</note> find God; rather, it brings a person into a vacancy that pagans, Jews, and all people, however evil they may be, can find—provided that they live in their sins without the reproach of conscience and can empty themselves of images and of all works.

<margin>In natural rest one cannot find God.</margin>

In this vacancy, the rest is pleasant and great, and this rest is not a sin in itself, for it exists in all people by nature, if only they knew how to empty themselves. But when one wishes to practice and possess this rest without the works of virtue, then the person falls into a <term>spiritual pride <note>original: *geestelijcke hoouerdie*; a specific vice where the practitioner mistakes their own psychological stillness for divine union.</note></term> and into a self-satisfaction from which one is seldom cured. And they often imagine that they have and are something, though they never actually arrive at it. When a person possesses this rest in vacancy in this manner, and every loving movement seems a hindrance to them, then they remain fixed in rest upon themselves, and live contrary to the first way that prepares a person for God—

But those who turned back upon themselves and sought rest in their own nature, taking pleasure in themselves within a natural light—that rest was brief and unlawful; they were blinded and separated from that eternal light, and they fell into darkness and eternal unrest.

## The Ornament

<margin>
God sometimes allows such people to be deceived.
</margin>
...[be]cause of the things they desire and indiscreetly demand from God, they are often deceived, and some even become possessed by the devil. These people live a life that is contrary to <term>charity <note>original: *charitate*; in this context, it refers to the theological virtue of selfless, divine love as opposed to mere human affection.</note></term> and to loving inwardness, in which a person offers himself and all he can do to the honor and love of God. Such a person can never be satisfied or find peace except in an <term>incomprehensible good <note>original: *onbegrijpelijck goet*; a spiritual reality that exceeds human understanding or grasp.</note></term>, which is God alone. For charity is a <term>bond of love <note>original: *minnen bant*; a mystical connection that draws the soul out of itself and into union with the Divine.</note></term> that carries us across, where we deny ourselves and are united with God, and God with us. But natural love always focuses back on itself and its own comfort, and it always remains alone. Nevertheless, natural love is so

<margin>
In what way natural love is similar to divine love and in what way it is different.
</margin>
similar to divine love on the outside—like two hairs on a single head—but their intentions are different. For the good person always seeks, intends, and desires the honor of God with an uplifted heart; but in natural love, the person always intends himself and his own profit. But whenever natural love overcomes charity in these contrary ways, the person falls into four sins: namely, spiritual pride, greed, gluttony...

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>Ruusbroec distinguishes between divine charity, which unites the soul with God through self-denial, and "natural love," which deceptively mimics holiness while actually seeking self-interest. He warns that focusing on personal comfort rather than God leads to spiritual deception and the onset of vice.</summary>

### CHAPTER LXXVII.

->Of the errors of false emptiness which are contrary to the three modes and all virtue.<-

Whenever a person possesses natural rest in emptiness and in all their works intends only themselves and remains firmly unrepentant in <term>self-centeredness <note>original: *eygentheyt*; a state of being "owned" by oneself rather than God, characterized by ego and spiritual attachment.</note></term>, they cannot be united with God; for they live without <term>charity <note>original: *charitate*; divine, selfless love.</note></term> in unlikeness to Him. And here begins the third mode, which is contrary to all virtue and is the most harmful of all; and this is an unrighteous life full of spiritual errors and all perversity. Now pay close attention to this so that you may understand it well.

<margin>
How one shall recognize those who live in false emptiness.
</margin>

The people who lead this life imagine that they are <term>contemplative <note>original: *Godtschouwende*; literally "God-beholding."</note></term> people and the holiest who live; nevertheless, they live in opposition and unlikeness to God, all the saints, and good people. Now observe this meaning so you may recognize them both in words and in works. In the first place, then, because of the natural rest which they feel and possess within themselves in emptiness, they maintain that they are free, and united with God without intermediary, and that they are raised above all practices of the Holy Church and above the commandments of God, above the law, and above all virtuous works which one [must do] in eternal

<meta>catchword: ...nity</meta>

<summary>Ruusbroec warns against "false emptiness," a state where individuals mistake natural psychological rest for divine union, leading to spiritual pride and the rejection of religious laws and moral duties.</summary>
<keywords>false emptiness, quietism, self-centeredness, charity, contemplation, spiritual error, mystical union, Jan van Ruusbroec</keywords>

Book III transitions into the "superessential" or "contemplative" life, the highest stage of mystical union where the soul is drawn beyond its own being into the life of God.</meta>

->The Ornament<-

# The Third Book of the Spiritual Marriage of the enlightened teacher <term>John Ruysbroeck <note>original: *Ioann Ruysbrochij*; the Latinized form of Jan van Ruysbroeck (1293–1381), a Flemish mystic.</note></term>, speaking of the superessential contemplative life.

## CHAPTER I.

### How one enters into a God-contemplating life by means of three points.

<image-desc>Large ornamental historiated initial D, featuring intricate floral and foliate scrollwork within a rectangular border.</image-desc>

And for this reason, these solitary contemplative people shall go out according to the mode of contemplation, above reason and above distinction, and above their created being with an eternal inward-gazing <note>original: *insterren*; literally "in-staring," a technical term for the fixed, unwavering gaze of the soul upon God.</note>. By means of that in-born light, they are thus transformed and made one with the same light with which they see and which they see. And thus these contemplative people pursue their eternal image after which they were made; and they behold God and all things without distinction in a simple seeing within the divine clarity. # The Ornament

...and may increase in the heights of life in every loving inward-turning <note>original: *minnelijcken inkeer*; the soul’s act of withdrawing from external things to focus on the internal presence of God.</note> above all that can be understood; for he remains free and master of himself in inwardness and in virtues. And that beholding <term>beholding: *schauwe* or *schouwen*; the act of contemplative vision or "gazing" where the soul perceives God directly without the use of images or reasoning.</term> in that divine light holds him above all inwardness, and above all virtues, and above all merit; for it is the crown and reward for which we yearn, and which we now have and possess in a certain way; for a contemplative life is a Heavenly life <margin>How we may live a Heavenly life.</margin>; but were we released from this world, we would be more capable, according to our created nature, of receiving that clarity, and so the glory of God might shine through us more perfectly and more nobly in every way. This is the mode above all modes <note>original: *wyse bouen alle wyse*; refers to a state where human methods and "ways" of acting are superseded by God’s own "wayless" essence.</note>, how one goes out into a divine beholding, and into an eternal dying <note>original: *eeuwich steruen*; the mystical concept of the soul "dying" to its own self-will and identity to be completely absorbed in God.</note>, and how one is transformed and over-formed <note>original: *ghetransformeert en ouerformt*; "over-formed" suggests being reshaped or transfigured into a higher state of being by the divine light.</note> into divine clarity. This "going out" of contemplative people is also loving, for through enjoyable love <note>original: *ghebruyckelijcke minne*; the specific type of love that rests in and "enjoys" God's presence, rather than just seeking Him through labor or virtue.</note> he transcends his created nature and finds and tastes the richness and the abundance which God Himself is, and which He causes to flow without ceasing into the hiddenness of the spirit, where he stands like the nobility of God.

<meta>catchword: CHAPTER</meta>

<summary>This page describes the pinnacle of the contemplative life, where the soul transcends its own virtues and created nature to be "over-formed" by divine clarity. Ruusbroec explains that this state of "eternal dying" allows the soul to taste the very richness of God's essence within its own hidden spirit.</summary>
<keywords>contemplative life, divine clarity, inwardness, transformation, eternal dying, enjoyable love, mystical union, Jan van Ruusbroec</keywords>

## CHAPTER VI.

### Concerning a Divine Meeting that is Practiced in the Secret Places of Our Spirit.

When the inward, contemplative person <note>original: *innigē schouvvendē mensche*; refers to one who has turned their focus entirely inward to gaze upon God.</note> has thus followed his eternal image, and in this purity has possessed the Father’s bosom through the Son; then he is transfigured <note>original: *verclaert*; meaning enlightened or made clear by divine light.</note> with divine truth, and he receives the eternal birth anew at every hour, and he goes out according to the mode of the light into a divine beholding and a loving meeting, in which our highest blessedness is situated above all things.

<margin>
How the Father is turned toward the Son and the Son toward the Father.
</margin>
You shall know that the Heavenly Father, as a living ground, with all that lives in Him, is actively turned toward His Son, as into His own eternal wisdom. And the same wisdom, and all that lives within her, is actively bent back <note>original: *vvederboocht*; a mystical term describing the "refluence" or the return of the Divine Persons to their shared essence.</note> into the Father, that is, into the same ground from which she comes. And in this meeting the third person springs forth between the Father and the Son, that is, the Holy Spirit, the love of them both, who is one with them both in the same nature. And he embraces and permeates actively and enjoyably <term>enjoyably: *gebruyckelijc*; relating to "fruition," the state where the soul or the Divine Persons rest in the "enjoyment" of their essence.</term> the Father

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>This page explores the mystical "meeting" within the Trinity, describing how the contemplative soul participates in the eternal cycle of the Father turning toward the Son and the Holy Spirit emerging as their mutual love.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Jan van Ruusbroec, Contemplation, Holy Spirit, Eternal Birth, Mystical Union, Divine Wisdom</keywords>

57. How we shall meet God spiritually with an intermediary and without an intermediary <note>original: *met middel ende sonder middel*; "with intermediary" refers to knowing God through means like scripture or nature; "without intermediary" refers to a direct, mystical union of essence.</note>.

58. Of the essential meeting of God in bare nature <note>original: *blooter naturen*; the soul’s essence when stripped of all images, thoughts, and created things.</note> that is without an intermediary.

59. How one is like God through grace, and unlike [God] through sin.

60. How one possesses God in unity and in rest above all likeness of grace.

61. How the grace of God is necessary, which makes us like [Him] and leads us into God without an intermediary.

Ruusbroec uses this to criticize a specific group of contemporary "quietists" who sought a state of passive, natural emptiness without the active love of God, which he considered a dangerous heresy.</note>, and how some people who follow this are contrary to these three aforementioned ways, and this concerns the first.

<meta>This page contains the errata (printing errors) for the preceding text. It follows the Table of Contents for Jan van Ruusbroec’s work, likely provided at the end of the volume to correct mistakes made during the typesetting process. The corrections focus on significant mystical terms, such as the distinction between "created" and "uncreated" light, which are crucial to Ruusbroec's theology.</meta>

# ERRATA IN THE LIFE
<note>original: *DRVCK FAVTEN INT LEVEN*; this refers to printing errors found within the biographical or main text section of the book.</note>

folio 55, line 1. General, read: the General.
f. 66, l. 26. devovred <note>original: *varslonden*</note>, read: devoured <note>original: *verslonden*</note>.

In the preface, f. 72, l. 13. gives out, read: spirit-going-forth. <note>original: *vutgeest*; a mystical term for the spirit’s movement toward the Divine.</note>
f. 73, l. 19. darkness, read: of the darkness.
f. 65, l. 16. and wherefore, read: the wherefore.
f. 67, l. [noted] she gives a spirit, read: they spirit-forth one spirit. <note>original: *sy ge-sten eenen geest*; "ghosting" or "spiriting" was an early modern way to describe spiritual action.</note>
f. 72, l. 1. by cross, read: he bore his cross.

f. 118, l. 23. who, read: this.
f. 121, l. 19. gives, read: spirit.
f. 164, l. 20. in this wise person, read: in this wise; remove "person".
f. 164, l. 21. in this, read: in this person.
f. 181, l. 10. and who did, read: and from those.
f. 181, l. 21. and among, read: and without.
f. 195, l. 12. now created light, read: in the created light. <term>created light: *geschapen licht*; in mystical theology, this refers to natural or angelic understanding, as opposed to the "uncreated light" of God Himself.</term>
f. 200, l. 17. to a created good, read: to an uncreated good. <note>This is a vital correction; "uncreated good" refers to God's essence.</note>
f. 204, l. 10. this kills love, read: this kills the lover. <note>original: *de minnende*</note>
f. 209, l. 22. the immage <note>original: *de belde*</note>, read: the image <note>original: *den beelde*</note>.

Chapter 60. Here the title is forgotten, which is: **How one possesses God in unity and rest above all likeness of the graces.**
f. 218, l. 18. eternal ground, read: simple ground. <note>original: *eenvuldigẽ gront*; "simple" here means pure, undivided, and absolute.</note>
f. 218, l. 25. above thing, read: above all things.
f. 228, l. 8. is the foundation, read: he is the foundation.

<summary>This page lists printing errors and their corrections for the preceding manuscript, including a significant forgotten chapter title and corrections to theological terms like "created light" and "uncreated good."</summary>
